Victims of Nazi war in Poland call on Germany for support

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

More than 80 years after the end of World War II, Polish victims of the Nazi regime are still waiting for a "humanitarian gesture" from Germany. The 2027 draft budget hasn't left them much hope for possible reparations.

"We can no longer wait," declared Polish victims' organizations in a dramatic appeal to German civil society, published on November 5 on the website of the Foundation for Polish–German Reconciliation.

"Around 50,000 survivors of Nazi persecution during the German occupation still live in Poland today. They include former concentration camp and prison inmates, people deported to Germany for forced labor and those subjected to other forms of Nazi repression," wrote the signatories of the appeal.

The open letter pointed out that many of the survivors are over 90 years old, struggling with illness and rising costs, and in need of help in their daily lives. "For many, any further delay in deciding to provide assistance means that help will come too late," said the appeal, which was distributed in German, Polish and English.

While Germany has recognized its moral responsibility toward the remaining survivors of Nazi persecution, the signatories pointed out that a corresponding decision on possible reparations by the German federal government has yet to be made.

"Today, we appeal primarily to German civil society to provide humanitarian support to survivors of Nazi persecution living in Poland," they wrote, addressing businesses, foundations, associations and private citizens. "Every donation matters."

The unresolved issue of war reparations has clouded German-Polish relations for decades.

Following the 1989 democratic transition in Central and Eastern Europe and the reunification of Germany, the federal government provided 500 million deutschmarks to Holocaust victims in Poland. More than 1 million people received support through these funds. Former forced laborers also received payments from the EVZ Foundation, which works to preserve the memory of the victims of National Socialist persecution.

However, given the scale of German crimes during World War II, the reparations provided to date have been considered as insufficient by large parts of Polish society. German occupation policy in Poland was characterized by systematic crimes against the Polish population. An estimated 6 million Polish citizens were killed during the war, including about 3 million Polish Jews.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

As part of the "General Plan East," a large portion of the population was to be displaced, enslaved or murdered. As early as 1939, the "Intelligenzaktion" specifically targeted the Polish elites, including teachers, clergy, civil servants and intellectuals. In addition, approximately 2.5 million Poles were deported to the German Reich for forced labor and made to work there under inhumane conditions.

When it came to power in 2015, Poland's right-wing conservative Law and Justice Party (PiS)  made the issue of war reparations a central focus of Warsaw's policy toward Berlin. The PiS government estimated Poland's war losses at 6.2 trillion zloty (approx. €1.3 trillion/$1.58 trillion) and officially demanded this sum from Berlin in October 2022.

The center-left government led by Donald Tusk has distanced itself from these demands since Tusk's election victory in December 2023. However, the government has also made no secret of the fact that Poland is expecting a "creative solution" from its German partner, according to Foreign Minister Radek Sikorski.

Former German Chancellor Olaf Scholz had offered €200 million ($224 million) prior to his visit to Poland in the summer of 2024, an offer rejected by Tusk as insufficient. The talks held since then between Berlin and Warsaw have failed to produce a solution.

In a final declaration following the German-Polish government consultations in December 2025, the German government promised to "analyze possibilities for additional support for Polish victims of Nazi aggression and the German occupation of Poland."

"Currently, there is no decision regarding a humanitarian gesture and the draft budget reflects this status," Josef Hinterseher, deputy spokesperson for Germany's Federal Foreign Office, told reporters on September 23.

Germany's opposition Greens party has not given up hope of a turnaround in its efforts to secure a "humanitarian gesture."

"Our parliamentary group submitted a request to the budget committee to secure funding for this 'humanitarian gesture.' Unfortunately, the request was rejected," Franziska Brantner, co-chair of the Greens, told DW on Thursday in Warsaw.

"Hope dies last, so we will do everything in our power to ensure that the coalition makes this change after all," she emphasized. Brantner sees the budget committee's "final review session," which will take place this week, as a last chance.

The issue of reparations still plays an important role in Polish society, even more than 80 years after the end of the war. There is hardly a family that did not suffer during the war. In the latest German-Polish Barometer — a representative survey that has been conducted for 25 years — 53% of respondents stated that the suffering of the Polish people is not sufficiently acknowledged in Germany. Only 18% believed that the Germans have done enough.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Jakub Deka, chairman of the Foundation for Polish–German Reconciliation, believes the appeal by the victims' associations could now mobilize Germany's civil society. Following an initial appeal in May directed at the German federal government, several hundred Germans responded with donations ranging from €50 to several hundred euros.

To date, a total of more than €35,000 ($39,000) has been deposited into the foundation's account. Several people in need have already received one-time assistance from this fund. "It's a drop in the bucket, but every little bit helps," Deka told DW.

The foundation's chairman quotes Jerzy Tarasiewicz — who led the victims' association in the Polish city of Gdansk for many years — as a cautionary reminder. In January, Tarasiewicz was quoted in an interview with the daily newspaper Gazeta Wyborcza as saying: "They [the Germans] are waiting for us to die."

Tarasiewicz, who was born in 1942 on a German farm where his parents were performing forced labor, died in late September at the age of 84 without being able to witness this "humanitarian gesture."

"It is a painful reminder that the victims cannot wait any longer," said Deka.

This article was originally written in German.

Spain's king and queen attend memorial service for rail crash victims

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Spain's King Felipe VI and Queen Letizia were greeted by bishops as they arrived at the funeral mass on Tuesday

Spain's King Felipe and Queen Letizia were among those who have attended a memorial service in tribute to the 69 victims of last month's deadly train crash.

The accident occurred when one train, travelling at high speed, ploughed into the back of another that was stationary on the Madrid-Seville line, near the small town of Urda, about 40 miles from Toledo.

It was the worst rail tragedy in Spain since 2013. Twenty-eight people remain in hospital.

"The tragic accident in Urda has left our families, our towns and our society with a pain that is difficult to accept," Archbishop Cerro Chaves told those present.

Representatives of those killed carried lit candles to the front of the cathedral and placed them next to the main altar. The first reading of the service was by Ana BenÃ­tez, sister of David BenÃ­tez, a 38-year-old father-of-two who died.

The king and queen were met with applause by bystanders as they entered the cathedral and at the end of the service, after Catholic Mass, they spoke to relatives of the victims.

Senior politicians also attended the service, including the leader of the opposition People's Party, Alberto NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o, the president of the Castilla-La Mancha region, Emiliano GarcÃ­a-Page, and Transport and Economy Minister Carlos Cuerpo.

Cuerpo took on the transport portfolio following the resignation last week of Ãscar Puente from the post.

The Archbishop of Toledo officiated the memorial service

Puente had come under mounting pressure following the crash, Spain's second major rail tragedy this year after 46 people died in a collision on the same high-speed line in January.

Some local people from the town of Adamuz in Andalusia, where that accident happened, as well as residents of Urda, were present for the memorial service.

The investigation into the recent accident is still in progress. Puente suggested that human error combined with a glitch in the safety system may have been the cause.

The tragedy happened a month before the 29 November general election and is seen as likely to hurt Socialist Prime Minister Pedro SÃ¡nchez's attempts to secure re-election.

The ceremony took place on the same day as a nationwide strike by train drivers, who are demanding greater safety on the rail network.

Rail services were also expected to be affected across the country on Wednesday due to a general strike, led by housing activists calling for urgent measures to control rental costs.

Violence erupts in Bangladesh as ousted PM's supporters stage protest

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Protesters have lit fires in the middle of roads

Police in Bangladesh have fired tear gas and rubber bullets to disperse supporters of the deposed Prime Minister Sheikh Hasina, who are demanding her return to the country.

A police vehicle was set on fire in Gopalganj district, where the banned student wing of Hasina's Awami League party clashed with security forces.

Nationwide protests by pro-Hasina activists started a day after the former leader said she had requested a passport to return to Bangladesh from exile in India.

The demonstrations by the student wing, called Bangladesh Chhatra League (BCL), are seen as the first organised show of force by Hasina's supporters since she was ousted in August 2024 following a huge uprising against her rule.

Sheikh Hasina was ousted in 2024 following weeks of mass protests

Traffic was disrupted in some parts of the country as protesters tried to set up roadblocks using burning tyres.

Several passenger vehicles were also set on fire in various parts of Bangladesh in the run up to the protest.

A 60-year-old driver of a passenger bus was killed by smoke inhalation after people set fire to the vehicle in the city of Faridpur early on Tuesday.

Elsewhere, about 200 protesters briefly blocked traffic on the 6.2km (4 mile)-long Padma bridge across the Ganges, that connects the capital Dhaka with the south-western region.

Bangladeshi local media said dozens of crude bombs exploded in the past few days in Dhaka and elsewhere.

"Any attempt to worsen the law-and-order situation or create instability through any conspiracy within the country from across the border (India) will not be allowed to succeed," Bangladesh Home Minister Salahuddin Ahmed said.

"Our supporters were not responsible for the violent incidents. They were carried out by unknown miscreants. We believe in peaceful protests," a senior leader of the BCL, who is now in exile in a foreign country, said.

Hasina fled to neighbouring India two years ago following a massive uprising, bringing an end to her 15-year reign.

Opposition parties accused Hasina of jailing political rivals, supressing dissent and overseeing widespread human rights abuses. She always denied the charges.

Student-led demonstrations against job quotas escalated into mass protests in 2024 after a bloody police crackdown

The United Nations has estimated up to 1,400 people were killed, most by gunfire from security forces, during the anti-government protests in 2024.

The activities of the Awami League were banned soon after she fled the country and it was not allowed to contest the February general election.

A special Bangladeshi tribunal last year sentenced her to death in absentia after being found guilty of crimes against humanity.

She has denied all the charges, calling the trial "biased and politically motivated".

Despite the death penalty threat, Hasina said she wanted to return to Bangladesh in December and requesteded the Bangladesh High Commission in New Delhi to issue her with a passport.

But the Bangladeshi government said the country would not provide a passport to her and called her "a terrorist".

"She is a fugitive criminal. We have an active extradition treaty with India. We want her handed over," Humaiun Kobir, Bangladesh's junior foreign affairs minister, said.

"Should we treat her like an ordinary commercial traveller and tell her to come and visit her in-laws' residence?" Kobir asked.

A state prosecutor in Bangladesh on Tuesday said the verdict would be executed if she came back, saying she had missed the opportunity to appeal.

Awami League leaders allege that in the past two years hundreds of supporters have been killed in mob violence and dozens have died in the custody of security forces.

Dhaka officials reject accusations that Awami League supporters are being systematically targeted.

'Europe not going anywhere': G7 summit a 'signal to Russians' that Europe is standing behind Ukraine

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

As the US Midterms reshape Ukraine diplomacy, Annette Young is pleased to welcome James Townsend, Atlantic Council's Senior Advisor at the Scowcroft Center's for Transatlantic Security Initiative. "Europe is not going anywhere." With this unequivocal assessment, Townsend, taking place alongside trilateral negotiations in Abu Dhabi. While the latter may command international attention, Townsend argues that the Paris meeting carries a message of its own: European support for Ukraine is intended to remain durable, and Moscow should not mistake diplomatic negotiations for an erosion of Western resolve. The G7 meeting therefore emerges as more than a forum for diplomatic declarations. It represents a test of Western cohesion, the credibility of future military and financial assistance, and Washington's willingness to endorse a common position on Ukraine. As Townsend emphasizes, the final communiqué will also be "a test as far as what Marco Rubio is going to agree to put his name to."

Israel's new parliament sworn in as leaders compete to form government

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Gadi Eisenkot has vowed to oust Benjamin Netanyahu, but still has to get enough parties on board

Israel's new parliament has been sworn in, as opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot seeks to oust caretaker Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu.

The former army chief has been tasked with forming a government after receiving more recommendations from members of the Knesset than Netanyahu, although not all those backing him are willing to govern together.

Eisenkot's bloc of four parties won 48 seats in October's election, while Netanyahu's bloc won 53, leaving both short of a majority in the 120-seat Knesset. He has 28 days to build a coalition, with a possible two-week extension.

Almost half of the new MKs did not serve in the previous parliament, while Arab-led parties have a record number of seats.

Shortly after they were sworn in, Netanyahu and Eisenkot warned parliament about the perils of supporting a government led by the other.

"[And] to those who are thinking of establishing a government that includes supporters of terror and haters of Israel, or that will be dependent on them, I ask you, are you prepared to be dependent on those whose goal is the destruction of the Jewish state?" said Netanyahu, alluding to the prospect of an Eisenkot-led government reliant on support from Arab parties.

He also appealed for the formation of a government of national unity as the only way to confront what he said was the threat of "a nuclear Iran".

Eisenkot sharply criticised Netanyahu's comments.

"I sat and listened to the outgoing prime minister's delusional, divisive, hate-filled and shameful speech, exploiting a state occasion," he said. "I am now even more convinced that he is unfit to remain in office for even one more minute."

The outcome of the election, which was widely seen as a referendum on Netanyahu, means there is a period of uncertainty over who will form the next government. Netanyahu will remain caretaker prime minister until then - a process which could take from days to months.

One of the most dramatic results was the performance of Israel's two Arab-led alliances - the United Arab List and the Joint List - which won an unprecedented 19 seats between them following a surge in turnout among Arab voters.

The Arab parties could therefore hold the balance of power, although Netanyahu has ruled out governing with them and three of the parties in Eisenkot's bloc have opposed including them in a coalition.

On Sunday, President Isaac Herzog tasked Eisenkot with trying to form a government.

Speaking at the president's residence, the centrist Yashar party leader called for the formation of a "national emergency government" - an implicit appeal to parties to put ideological differences aside to remove Netanyahu from office.

This, he said, would "require creative and unconventional political steps".

Eisenkot said any party or MK prepared to accept four principles could join his proposed government. Those are recognition of Israel as a Jewish and democratic state; accepting the values of the Declaration of Independence; backing mandatory military, national or civilian service for all, including communities currently exempt; and recognising Hamas as a terrorist organisation and committing to dismantling its military and governing capabilities.

The four requirements, however, could present significant obstacles to Eisenkot's potential partners.

The conscription of ultra-Orthodox Jewish men, who are exempt from military service if they are in full-time religious study, was a major election issue.

The ultra-Orthodox United Torah Judaism (UTJ) and Shas parties, which won 13 seats in total, have fiercely objected to attempts to change the status quo - something that all parties in Eisenkot's bloc campaigned to do.

Eisenkot's four principles are also contentious for the Arab parties. Both emphasise equal national and civic rights for Israel's Arab citizens, who form about 20% of the population; the vast majority of Israeli Arabs are not required to serve in the military; and neither has publicly recognised Hamas as a terrorist organisation.

The Arab parties could, though, help Eisenkot form a minority government. The United Arab List, which won eight seats, has said it is willing to be part of an Eisenkot-led coalition, while the Joint List, which won 11 seats, has offered parliamentary support from the outside.

If Eisenkot accepted such an arrangement, he would still have to persuade other parties in his bloc to agree. Avigdor Lieberman, whose Yisrael Beiteinu party won seven seats, has so far refused to join a minority government dependent on Arab MKs.

In return for their support, the ultra-Orthodox parties would be likely to demand major concessions on military conscription, which might be unacceptable to most of the parties in Eisenkot's bloc.

Malaysia's disgraced former PM leaves jail for house arrest after embezzling millions

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Najib smiles to reporters at the Kuala Lumpur High Court after it cleared the way for him to go home

Malaysia's disgraced former Prime Minister Najib Razak has arrived home to begin serving the rest of a jail sentence for corruption under house arrest.

There was a big media presence at his Kuala Lumpur residence to see the 73-year-old return from prison in an SUV late on Tuesday night, escorted by a heavily armed police convoy.

Najib's return ended a four-year prison stint after he was found guilty over the embezzlement of billions of dollars from the country's sovereign wealth fund.

The 1MDB scandal brought down his government and made headlines around the world when it came to light a decade ago, embroiling prominent figures from Malaysia to Goldman Sachs and Hollywood.

Investigators estimated that $4.5bn (Â£3.3bn) was siphoned from the state-owned wealth fund, which Najib co-founded, into private pockets, including his own. More than $1bn allegedly ended up in accounts linked to Najib, according to prosecutors.

Najib's lawyers claim he had been misled by his advisers - in particular the financier Jho Low, who has maintained his innocence and remains at large.

Journalists jostled for a glimpse of Najib as he was driven to his home, which has been declared a federal prison

Najib's mansion in one of the capital's most exclusive neighbourhoods was declared a federal prison ahead of his homecoming on Tuesday.

Critics said his release from jail undermined efforts to fight corruption and questioned its legal basis, saying Malaysian law made no provision for house arrest.

Najib, who was prime minister from 2009 to 2018, denied wrongdoing but was convicted at two separate trials.

In the first trial he was sentenced in 2020 to 12 years in prison and jailed in 2022. The term was later halved on appeal.

In September, the King of Malaysia granted him a conditional pardon to see out the remaining two years of his sentence at home.

The transfer to his mansion was to take place on Friday but put on hold after the attorney general sought clarification of the royal decree. On Tuesday the high court ruled the house arrest was effective immediately.

Najib's party paid a $12m fine as part of the terms of the royal pardon allowing him to serve the rest of his term at home.

He was sentenced after being convicted of abuse of power, money laundering and breach of trust over 42 million ringgit ($10m; Â£7.7m) transferred from SRC International - a former unit of 1MDB - into his private accounts.

Najib is appealing against a 15-year prison sentence, handed down at a second trial in December 2025, in a case involving much larger sums of money. In that case he was convicted of misappropriating nearly 2.3 billion Malaysian ringgit ($569m; Â£422m) from the nation's sovereign wealth fund 1Malaysia Development Berhad (1MDB).

He is due to start serving that sentence after his house arrest ends, which is expected in August 2028.

Meghan's father Thomas Markle back in federal custody after allegedly removing ankle monitor

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Thomas Markle was placed under house detention at his assisted living facility in Florida last week

Thomas Markle, the father of Meghan, Duchess of Sussex, is back in federal custody in Florida after allegedly removing his ankle monitor, according to court documents.

Markle appeared before a federal magistrate judge in the Florida city of Tampa on Tuesday and was denied bond. Prosecutors say he removed the monitor early that morning.

US Judge Lindsay S Griffin found on Tuesday that Markle had violated the conditions of his pretrial release, court documents show.

Markle, who has denied possessing images of child sex abuse, was arrested in Florida last week and placed under house detention. He was also ordered to wear a location monitoring device.

The BBC has contacted his representatives.

Griffin described Markle's housing situation as "untenable", writing in the order revoking his pretrial release that "based on the lack of suitable housing, no condition or combination of conditions would reasonably assure the safety of any other person or the community and the defendant's appearance in court".

He also ordered a medical evaluation for Markle and said he "must receive all necessary and prescribed medication unless discontinued by a physician".

"Release conditions are court orders, not suggestions," Bill Essayli, the US attorney for Central California, wrote on X on Tuesday.

"We will continue to prosecute this case to the fullest extent of the law," he added.

Markle, 82, was first arrested last week after the FBI, acting on warrants, searched storage units in Los Angeles registered to him which he had rented since 2008, with the last payment being made in October this year, the DoJ said.

Inside the units the FBI says agents seized about 78 items, including film negatives, slides and books that they allege contained child sex abuse material.

Witnesses "positively identified" Markle in two of the images believed to contain child sex abuse material.

In those images, the man pictured "appears to be in the age range of 30s to 40s", which suggests the alleged abuse dates back decades as Markle is currently 82 years old.

Markle appeared in court last week following his arrest. No plea was taken during the appearance, which set several conditions for his pretrial release, including that he be kept in home detention.

He is charged with one count of possessing indecent images of children, according to the DoJ.

FBI director Kash Patel said last week that Markle "allegedly targeted innocent children and sought to steal their innocence through his criminal actions".

Markle has called the allegations "sick lies".

He moved to the Philippines last year after he had a series of strokes, and lived in Cebu until May. He was reportedly learning to walk again in Florida after having his leg amputated.

The duchess has been estranged from her father since 2018. The BBC understands she broke contact with him last year, telling him she and the authorities knew he had abused young girls.

In a previously shared statement, Meghan confirmed the existence of the letter and wrote: "I am deeply distressed... my thoughts are with any children who may have been harmed."

She said she hoped it could be understood why she had largely cut off contact from her father and did "not allow him to have contact with our children".

The US military seems to be getting nervous about the launch supply crunch

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

“Launch is extremely constrained and that is exactly the situation we predicted.”

The US military is taking a new approach to prop up commercial launch companies as SpaceX looks to wind down its Falcon rocket program, suggesting defense officials see the workhorse rocket’s retirement as a potential national security risk.

The Pentagon’s Office of Strategic Capital has twice in the last two weeks announced it was sending cash to two of SpaceX’s competitors in the launch business. First, on October 26, the office announced a $150 million conditional loan commitment to Stoke Space. Military officials revealed a second $150 million Pentagon loan Tuesday for Rocket Lab.

Stoke Space and Rocket Lab are the first two launch companies to receive significant support from the Office of Strategic Capital (OSC), an organization established in 2022 to infuse companies developing “vital” national security technologies with capital. OSC has been on a loan spree in recent weeks, committing hundreds of millions of dollars to companies specializing in batteries, drones, and semiconductors. In the space sector, the office also provided a $150 million loan to LeoLabs, a commercial satellite tracking company, for production and deployment of radar units.

SpaceX, the Pentagon’s leading launch provider, has stopped taking commercial orders for Falcon 9 launches after 2028 as the company shifts its focus to the next-generation, super-heavy-lift Starship rocket. Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy launches for government customers, including NASA and the US Space Force, are expected to continue a while longer. The Space Force’s launch contract with SpaceX allows the military to place Falcon 9 and Falcon Heavy orders through 2029, with the actual launches extending through 2033.

Then, if Starship is fulfilling its promise, SpaceX plans to retire Falcon for good. Starship may eventually be capable of launching national security payloads, but SpaceX’s near-term priority is using the rocket’s massive cargo capacity for its own Starlink Internet satellites and Starmind orbital data centers.

Military space officials have held to a policy of assured access to space since the 1990s, with the Pentagon paying to support at least two US rocket programs capable of launching national security satellites. The support previously came in the form of direct subsidies, development funding, and launch contracts at premium prices to give the government technical and financial insight into their launch providers, schedule priority on their launch manifests, and, in some cases, to simply keep them in business.

The Space Force’s mix of launch providers today consists of SpaceX’s Falcon family and United Launch Alliance’s Vulcan rocket, which has been grounded twice in the last two years by problems with its solid rocket boosters. ULA’s woes with Vulcan forced the Space Force to shift several national security missions to SpaceX.

Other launch providers aim to compete for their own slices of the growing pie of Space Force launch contracts. Space Systems Command, which oversees launch procurement for the Space Force, now forecasts the military will require up to 249 medium-to-heavy-lift launches through the early 2030s from its roster of launch contractors, more than double the command’s expectation just two years ago.

Alongside SpaceX and ULA, Space Systems Command has tapped Blue Origin, Rocket Lab, Stoke Space, Relativity Space, and Impulse Space for eligibility to compete for some of those national security launches. Blue Origin is the only company that has flown a rocket that could meet the Space Force’s requirements. Rocket Lab, Stoke Space, and Relativity Space are developing their own medium- and heavy-lift launch vehicles, while Impulse Space is working on in-space propulsion.

The loans from the Pentagon’s Office of Strategic Capital aim to get new medium-class rockets from Stoke Space and Rocket Lab ready to meet the military’s needs.

Stoke Space’s loan is tailored to support the buildout of the company’s second launch pad at Cape Canaveral Space Force Station, Florida. The second launch site would support flights of Stoke Space’s Nova Block 2 rocket, an upgraded version of the company’s fully reusable Nova Pathfinder, which is set to debut sometime next year.

“By supporting the construction of additional launch infrastructure, this commitment would enhance the overall capacity and resilience of the commercial space industrial base,” said Jordan Sebold, senior managing director of investments at OSC.

The loan to Rocket Lab will support the company’s efforts to increase the flight rate of its partially reusable Neutron rocket, which Rocket Lab says is also scheduled to make its first launch next year.

“The proposed loan would support faster vehicle delivery and processing and allow Rocket Lab to expand its fleet of maritime vessels with additional equipment for Neutron recovery and transport,” OSC said in a statement. “It would also enable manufacturing upgrades to increase the production of composite structures and engines, and support infrastructure that would reduce turnaround time between launches once the reusable rocket makes its operational debut.”

Both conditional loans are subject to “various financial, legal, technical, investment, due diligence, and other requirements,” OSC said.

Adam Spice, Rocket Lab’s chief financial officer, said Monday the Pentagon loan is on “very attractive terms” and “underscores the importance” of the military’s appetite for launch capacity.

“We always take it when we can get it,” Spice said in a quarterly earnings call. “It gives us additional capital to scale key parts of our business, Neutron in particular … I think this is a helpful indicator, a very positive indicator, about where everyone sees the demand constraint and how it can be unlocked, and capital is certainly a part of that.”

Rocket Lab also unveiled new pricing for the Neutron rocket—an increase from $55 million to $70 million per launch—in response to what the company said was a surge in bookings for the still-unproven launch vehicle. Rocket Lab said it now has a backlog of 117 launches on contract, including orders for Neutron missions and the company’s smaller Electron rocket.

“Launch is extremely constrained, and that is exactly the situation we predicted,” said Peter Beck, Rocket Lab’s founder and CEO.

With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Blade Runner 2099 Boss Clarifies Whether The Show Has Direct Ties To The OG Film And 2049

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

How 007 Director Denis Villeneuve Trolled Audiences Amid The James Bond Casting Brouhaha

Stranger Things Upcoming Prequel Series Is Hinting At A Twist That Has Me So Confused

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Blade Runner saga is set to continue amid the 2026 TV schedule, thanks to an upcoming limited series. Blade Runner 2099 presents the next chapter in this science-fiction-infused tale of humans and replicants and puts new protagonists at the forefront. Given what’s come before, it’s only natural to wonder whether the show has direct connections to Ridley Scott’s original 1982 film and Denis Villeneuve’s 2017 sequel, 2049. Well, the showrunner is now shedding some light on that front.

If it weren’t already clear due to the title, 2099 picks up fifty years after the events of the critically acclaimed Blade Runner 2049. That film left quite a few plot threads on the table, including the revelation that the late Eldon Tyrell gave replicants the ability to reproduce. Heading up the new show is Silka Luisa, who is a longtime fan of the franchise. Luisa recently spoke with SFX Magazine on CinemaBlend and was asked if events from 2049 would come into play. With that, she gave a very clear answer:

No, [Blade Runner 2049] is not directly connected to the show. What was an important early mandate was that audiences could come to the show not having seen the films, and this could stand alone. This could be an introduction to the world of Blade Runner, and it wouldn’t be dependent on you having watched 2049, you having watched the first Blade Runner. In that sense, we had to divorce ourselves from the narrative.

Why Edward James Olmos Says He Owes His Battlestar Galactica Legacy To Blade Runner

Some fans may be a tad disappointed to learn that replicant reproduction won’t be expanded upon during the events of the show. Yet Luisa’s “mandate” does indeed make a lot of sense. What she and her collaborators ultimately want is a show that fits snugly into the continuity but also doesn’t require watching the movies. (Of course, both of those films are excellent and deserve to be watched regardless.) In short, the Shining Girls creator is promising a series that works on its own, and that could do away with any trepidation newcomers may have about jumping in.

Blade Runner 2099 takes viewers to a Los Angeles that’s now fully controlled by replicants, and humans must essentially live within the lower levels of society. At the center of the story is Olwen, a Replicant Blade Runner (played by Michelle Yeoh) who is only days away from dying. She is eventually partnered with Marion Jessop to take on a case. However, unbeknownst to Olwen, “Marion” is actually a human named Cora (Hunter Schafer), who is only masquerading as a Replicant to achieve her own goals.

Per what’s been shared thus far, Yeoh and Schafer forged a strong bond on set not unlike Harrison Ford and Ryan Gosling did while making 2049. However, as far as we know, Yeoh didn’t accidentally punch Schafer in the face like Ford did to Gosling. In fact, Yeoh was protective of her co-star amid production and, in a truly mom-coded move, she even bought Schafer a baby helmet.

The trailers for 2099 would suggest that such headgear was necessary, because a lot of intense moments have been teased thus far. Although fans won’t see any direct links to the Blade Runner films, Silka Luisa is aiming to dish out the action-infused thrills, heightened visuals and deep themes that have long permeated this franchise.

Check out Blade Runner 2099 when all eight episodes are released for Prime Video subscription holders on November 25. In the meantime, anyone who’s curious can stream the original movie for free on Pluto TV and check out 2049 on Prime as well.

Patients admit lying to get antibiotics – and most say it worked

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A survey of 959 U.S. adults explores why some patients exaggerate or invent symptoms to obtain antibiotics, and how often they report success.

Many people walk into a clinic hoping to leave with antibiotics. In a new survey, more than a quarter of the American adults questioned said they had gone further.

To get antibiotics, they exaggerated how severe their symptoms were, said they had lasted longer than they had, or made up symptoms entirely.

Most of those who tried said it worked at least once.

Alistair Thorpe of University of Utah Health and Andrea Levy of CT State Community College led the survey, with Angela Fagerlin of Utah as the study’s senior author.

The 959 adults who answered came from an online survey panel rather than a random sample, and everything rests on what they admitted to. So the team called the numbers a first estimate rather than a national count.

“It is a tough situation,” Thorpe said. “People are experiencing unpleasant symptoms, and they can have this strong feeling that antibiotics are the only thing that can help them feel better.”

“Being told to just rest and wait is a hard thing to do, and if you think there’s something that could make you feel better, it’s a very hard impulse to override,” he added.

The survey asked two plain questions. Had the person ever told a health care provider that symptoms were worse or lasted longer than they did, in order to get antibiotics?

And had they ever claimed a symptom they didn’t have at all?

Of the 959 respondents, 268 said yes to at least one, and more than half of those had done both.

About one in four had stretched a real symptom, such as a cough reported as two weeks old when it was one week old.

About one in six had invented one, such as a fever that never happened.

Cold-like symptoms were the ones most often misrepresented, named by about two-thirds of the group. Stomach trouble and joint or muscle pain came next.

Of those 268 people, 206 said the tactic had earned them antibiotics at least once. Asked where they learned what to say, they most often named their own experience, then friends and family, then a clinician.

About one in five pointed to social media, and 31 said an AI chatbot had coached them.

Why do it? The reasons people ticked most often were plain. About 62% said they had felt really sick, and almost as many believed antibiotics were the only thing that would help.

About 61% wanted to get back to work or parenting faster, and 57% didn’t want to pass an illness to someone close to them.

Just over half said they knew their own body better than any provider. About 41% wanted spare antibiotics on hand for next time, and 40% saw no downside to taking them.

Antibiotics kill bacteria and do nothing against the viruses behind most colds and flu-like illnesses.

Every unneeded course also helps bacteria build resistance to the drugs, a problem researchers have traced from farm manure to the bacteria in human guts.

“These medications are not totally benign for the patient,” said Levy, a professor of social sciences. “And antibiotic use contributes to increasing antimicrobial resistance, which is a broader public health concern.”

Age mattered most. Adults under 56 had about three times the odds of reporting it as those 65 and older. Gender, race, and education were not linked to it.

Trust mattered too. People who often felt that clinicians didn’t take their symptoms seriously had about twice the odds of having misrepresented them.

People who endorsed at least one claim that antibiotic resistance is a hoax also had higher odds of reporting symptom misrepresentation.

Nearly a third of all respondents held such a belief, and one in five agreed that drug companies invented resistance to charge more.

Greater antibiotic knowledge was associated with lower odds of reporting symptom misrepresentation.

Those who said they needed help reading health materials had more than twice the odds.

Across the whole sample, 64% either thought antibiotics work on viruses or weren’t sure.

Thorpe said a past prescription can teach the wrong lesson. A viral infection clears on its own, and a person who was given antibiotics for one can credit the pills for the recovery.

Only 77 of the 268 said they regretted misrepresenting their symptoms, and 73 regretted taking the antibiotics they got.

The regrets people wrote in were more often about side effects or pills that didn’t help than about the lie itself.

Fewer than half said there was anyone they would listen to if told not to do it again.

The 691 respondents who said they had never done it gave their own reasons.

About 96% said honesty with a doctor matters so the doctor can treat them properly, 89% said they trust their clinician’s judgment, and 85% called lying morally wrong.

“There are very understandable reasons that we can sympathize with about why patients might feel the need to misrepresent symptoms, or why caregivers might do so on behalf of their children,” Levy said.

“We’re simply saying: perhaps there’s a better way.”

The panel was recruited with quotas for age, gender, race, and region, but people who sign up for paid online surveys are not a cross-section of the population.

The team said the figures should be read as a starting point for larger studies.

Self-reporting may have led to underestimates.

Some people may have hidden a lie, which would make the true share higher, and the question asked about “ever,” so faded memories may have cut it as well.

The survey also did not ask how often anyone did it or exactly what they said.

“Going forward, it will be important to test different ways to change the communication patterns between patients and their clinicians,” said Fagerlin, who chairs the Department of Population Health Sciences at Utah.

Future research could test whether helping patients feel heard encourages more honest conversations about symptoms and antibiotics.

The full study was published in the journal JAMA Network Open.

Student Game Development Competition Shut Down Because Kids Kept Submitting AI Slop

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

This will be the last year for the Australian STEM Videogame Challenge, a competition for young people, as the entries have grown overrun with AI-generated slop. According to a report in PC Gamer the event, which has been around for 12 years, will cease operations because it was getting too difficult to determine which games were made with AI and which were made entirely by the kids themselves.

The contest was open to students from elementary to high school, and required kids either working alone or in groups of up to four people to submit a game based on a chosen theme complete with a design document and a short trailer showing off the game. On Reddit, a parent of a child who regularly submitted games to the competition lamented the competition’s shutdown. “My teenage son and his friends entered the competition for several years, meeting up regularly, discussing ideas, and working together on a shared passion project,” the parent wrote. “It gave them something exciting to work on, and a common goal to strive towards outside of school. It also gave them confidence, and made them feel part of a wider community.”

In a statement to PC Gamer, Cathy Zemaitis, head of the Student Learning and Progress division at ACER, the company that puts on the challenge, said, “The decision was driven by the rapid and complex rise of generative AI in game development, making it harder to judge what students made themselves, and moving the program away from its original purpose.”

The “justification” for using AI in a professional game development setting is to essentially make games faster and more cheaply. One of the stated purposes of the challenge is to “enable students to develop real-world skills.” How is that accomplished using AI tools, especially at an age when core game development skills haven’t yet been learned? I’d imagine not very effectively. And it really sucks that the example adults are setting with the push for and normalization of generative AI is resulting in the end of a valuable opportunity for the next generation to develop a skill. We are eating the game development seed corn.

“I know as adults the world can be unfair, and we need to adapt,” the parent on Reddit wrote. “But this really hit home for me, because the victims this time are children. My son was visibly gutted when I told him the news, and it genuinely felt like part of his identity had been destroyed.”

Premium Economy On US Airlines: How The Big 3 Actually Compare

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Demand for premium air travel is growing as passengers are increasingly prioritizing comfort when booking flights. According to consumer research, around 21% of travelers now consider physical comfort a deciding factor when allocating their holiday budgets. In response to this growing demand, airlines are investing heavily in their premium products. However, business and first class tickets remain expensive for many travelers. This is where premium economy comes in.

The cabin offers passengers more comfort and additional amenities than standard economy without the considerably higher cost of business class. In recent years, its popularity has been growing. In fact, industry data shows that the share of travelers choosing premium economy has increased from 16% to 20% year over year. Many airlines have been investing in this cabin, and the three largest US carriers, American Airlines, Delta Air Lines, and United Airlines, are no exception. In this guide, we will explore how their products compare.

It is no secret to anyone that US legacy carriers were relatively slow to enter the premium economy market. For much of the 2010s, these airlines relied on extra-legroom economy products rather than introducing a separate premium economy cabin. In fact, this approach worked well in the domestic market, but several European and Asian airlines had already taken a different direction. They began offering dedicated premium economy cabins that featured wider seats, more legroom, and a distinct onboard service.

Therefore, to remain competitive, all three US airlines introduced their own premium economy products. American Airlines was the first major US carrier to offer a dedicated premium economy cabin, introducing the product on its Boeing 787-9 aircraft in 2016. United Airlines and Delta Air Lines subsequently followed, launching Premium Plus and Premium Select, respectively.

So, how do their products compare? In terms of seating, the three airlines offer fairly similar experiences, at least on paper. All three generally provide around 38 inches of seat pitch, which measures the distance between corresponding points on successive rows of seats. Seat widths are also comparable, typically ranging from approximately 18.5 to 19 inches, depending on the airline and aircraft type.

Let’s take a look at the oneworld member’s premium economy seats. American Airlines offers its premium economy cabin on its Boeing 777-300ER, 777-200, and 787-9 aircraft, as well as Boeing 787-8s operating international services and flights to Alaska. The airline primarily uses Collins Aerospace MiQ seats for its premium economy product, although the airline’s new premium-configured Boeing 787-9s feature the Recaro R5, which offers larger entertainment screens, adjustable footrests and leg rests, and privacy wings.

Overall, seat pitch is standardized at 38 inches (96.5 cm) across all aircraft types, while seat width ranges from 18.5 inches (47 cm) to 19 inches (48.3 cm). Screen sizes, however, vary by seat model. Aircraft equipped with Collins MiQ seats feature 11.5-inch (29.2 cm) screens, while the newer Boeing 787-9s featuring Recaro R5 seats offer larger 13.3-inch (33.8 cm) displays.

On eligible international flights, the Dallas/Fort Worth-based airline also offers chef-inspired meals and complimentary beer, wine, and spirits when full drink service is available. In addition, passengers on long-haul flights also receive an amenity kit including some skincare and basic travel essentials. Overall, the experience is the same as economy but with a thorough upgrade.

United Airlines currently takes the lead, but Delta and United continue to offer passengers compelling premium economy products.

Now let’s take a look at United Airlines’ premium economy seats, which it markets as “United Premium Plus”. The airline was the last of the Big Three US carriers to introduce a dedicated premium economy product, launching the cabin in 2019. Today, Premium Plus is available on most of United’s widebody aircraft, although there are some exceptions.

According to ch-aviation, United currently operates 37 Boeing 767-300ERs, 16 767-400ERs, 55 777-200ERs, 22 777-300ERs, 12 787-8s, 64 787-9s, and 21 787-10s. Of its 55 777-200ERs, 51 feature Premium Plus. The exceptions are the airline’s high-density Boeing 777 fleet, comprising 19 777-200s and four 777-200ERs, which lack a dedicated premium economy cabin.

As for the seats themselves, United uses the Collins Aerospace MiQ model, similar to American Airlines. These offer about 38 inches (97 cm) of pitch and measure between 18.5 and 19 inches (47–48 cm) wide. Passengers also receive an upgraded onboard dining service. Premium Plus meals are specially prepared and served on ceramic plates alongside metal cutlery, rather than the disposable packaging and utensils generally used in economy.

Delta Air Lines markets its premium economy cabin as “Delta Premium Select.” It first introduced the product in 2017 aboard its Airbus A350-900 on a flight from Detroit Metropolitan Wayne County Airport (DTW) to Tokyo Narita Airport (NRT), becoming the second major US carrier to offer a dedicated premium economy cabin. Since then, the SkyTeam member has gradually expanded Premium Select across its international network, including on some of its older aircraft, such as the Boeing 767-400ER.

In terms of seating, Delta’s product is quite similar to its rivals’, although it features the carrier’s distinctive diamond stitching pattern. Premium Select seats generally offer 38 inches (96.5 cm) of pitch and a width of approximately 18.5 to 19 inches (47–48 cm), depending on the aircraft. Passengers also benefit from adjustable leg rests and footrests, greater recline, larger entertainment screens, and memory foam cushions for additional comfort.

Beyond the seats, the Atlanta-based airline has built a reputation for its onboard service, and Premium Select offers several improvements over standard economy. Passengers receive complimentary beer, wine, and spirits as well as soft drinks and hot beverages. The dining service also varies depending on the route, although meals are generally served on reusable tableware rather than the disposable packaging commonly found in economy.

Overall, all three airlines offer a fairly similar premium economy experience. Unlike business class, carriers have fewer opportunities to differentiate their products, particularly when the seats are identical or closely comparable. However, all three are targeting the same group of travelers: those looking for a noticeable improvement over standard economy without paying business class fares.

Although US airlines were relatively late to introduce dedicated premium economy cabins, the product has become an increasingly important part of their business. For airlines, the appeal is also financial. Premium economy can generate around 33% more revenue per square foot of cabin space than standard economy. It can also generate approximately 6% more revenue per square foot than business class, partly because the seats take up less space and the cost of providing the onboard service is generally lower.

This helps explain why the three carriers continue to invest in their premium economy products. American Airlines is expanding the cabin across its fleet, including on its newer 787-9 Dreamliners and Airbus A321XLRs. Delta Air Lines is also placing greater emphasis on premium seating. In the second quarter of 2026, the airline’s premium ticket revenue exceeded Main Cabin ticket revenue for the first time. Looking ahead, its upcoming A350-1000 aircraft are expected to feature more than 100 premium seats, including nearly 50 in Premium Select alone.

We are also seeing a similar trend elsewhere, as airlines increasingly add premium economy cabins to their aircraft. For instance, Emirates is rapidly expanding its premium economy offering across its fleet, while Air France has doubled the number of premium economy seats on some of its retrofitted 777-300ERs. Similarly, Cathay Pacific has increased the cabin’s capacity from 34 to 48 seats on its updated 777s, representing a 41% increase.

Other airlines such as Turkish Airlines are also preparing to enter the market. The airline is planning to introduce dedicated premium economy cabins from 2028. This comes as passenger preferences are changing, especially at the higher end of the market. Looking at the wider industry, traditional first class has become increasingly rare now because airlines have shifted their focus towards business class and premium economy. In the US, American is the last of the Big Three to retain a dedicated international first class product through its Flagship First cabin, although it is now phasing it out.

Elsewhere, first class remains concentrated among a relatively small number of international airlines, particularly on routes connecting major financial centers and through hubs in the Gulf. One reason for this shift is the improvement in business class products. Many airlines now offer fully lie-flat seats, greater privacy, and other amenities that were once associated primarily with first class. At the same time, premium economy has attracted passengers who want more comfort than standard economy but are unwilling to pay significantly higher business class fares.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

Those who are willing to spend more on business class continue to do so, but premium economy has created another option for travelers who previously had to choose between economy and business. According to Going’s 2026 State of Travel and Flight Deals Report, premium economy was the fastest-growing cabin choice among surveyed travelers, increasing from 16% to 20% year-on-year.

31 Years Later, The Line That Changed The X-Files Is Still A Work Of Genius

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Faith Roswell is a Senior Writer on Screen Rant's Classic TV team. Since earning her degree in Creative Writing over a decade ago, Faith has written articles on film and TV from a variety of different angles. Faith now combines her knowledge of psychology with her love of monster movies to give more insight into what makes the best ones.  You may have read her Screen Rant lists and features covering horror, sci-fi, and fantasy, or read her Amazon Top 10 book, "Movie Monsters of the Deep." Faith has had an extensive career as a writer, appearing on BBC live radio, researching true crime for Rotten Mango podcast, and writing for publications including Mental Floss, Atlas Obscura, and The Daily Jaws before beginning here at Screen Rant.

The X-Files' impact on both the sci-fi genre and culture is arguably second only to that of Star Trek. Through Dana Scully, "The Scully Effect" encouraged more women to pursue careers in the sciences, while the iconic 90s sci-fi series popularized the monster-of-the-week format, which inspired later TV shows like Warehouse 13 and Supernatural. The X-Files' classic episodes were never predictable, but viewers knew to expect a lineup of monsters, mutants, out-of-control computers, and other paranormal phenomena with every season, until one season 3 episode expanded The X-Files' scope with two words.

The fourth episode of The X-Files' season 3 sees Mulder and Scully investigating a string of gory murders targeting psychics, when they come across the tormented Clyde Bruckman, who claims that he can see how everyone he ever meets will die. The premise initially sounded fairly standard for The X-Files, but the episode, "Clyde Bruckman's Final Repose," contained a shocking twist. After Scully spends the episode trying to debunk Clyde's psychic powers, curiosity gets the better of her, and she asks him how she will die. Clyde responds, "you don't."

The line turned the series into an existential and spiritual mystery that had viewers discussing its implications for years afterward, even after The X-Files continued with its monster-of-the-week format for some time afterward. This moment, and the standout acting and directing kept "Clyde Bruckman's Final Repose" among the best X-Files episodes of all time, and the line's eventual payoff completed a four-year masterclass in suspense.

According to IMDb, "Clyde Bruckman's Final Repose" is The X-Files' top-rated episode, earning a 9.2/10 mark, and it attracted an audience of 15.38 million viewers. A few episodes of the show have not aged well, but "Clyde Bruckman's Final Repose" is still among the X-Files episodes that are considered masterpieces today. Written by Darin Morgan and directed by David Nutter, who would later go on to win an Emmy Award for Game of Thrones, the episode is extremely dark, verging into film noir territory, and many of the references are hidden tributes to cinema's silent film era.

The genius of the episode is not just in the plot, but the way Mulder and Scully are developed further as characters in ways that are not easy to achieve within a monster-of-the-week format. While remaining the show's skeptic, Scully has previously been sensitive to influence by psychics, with "Beyond the Sea" flipping her and Mulder's roles in the show. Now, she remains unconvinced by the psychic aspect but sees Clyde as a tragic and human figure, which allows her to set aside the cold and scientific demeanor she sometimes puts on, while Mulder treats him as a case study.

"Clyde Bruckman's Final Repose" won The X-Files two Emmy Awards. Darin Morgan won Outstanding Writing in a Drama Series, while Peter Boyle (Clyde) won Outstanding Guest Actor in a Drama Series.

The episode is full of tiny unpredictable scenes and surprises for viewers, introducing a dark comedy moment when Clyde heavily implies that Mulder will die during a sexual mishap, but it was Clyde's line to Scully that reportedly sent fans into a frenzy, with the "Immortal Dana Scully" theory being the inevitable result. It is a testament to the quality of the episode that it is remembered for so many factors, from the acting to the heartbreaking ending, despite delivering the line that changed The X-Files, even though this line was later revealed to be a misunderstanding.

Four years after Clyde Bruckman appeared to confirm Scully's immortality, the season 6 episode "Tithonus" reinforced this by having her survive against impossible odds. While investigating Alfred Fellig, a seemingly-immortal photographer on a mission to photograph Death, Scully is shot, yet Fellig takes her place. By this time, The X-Files had already begun making references to events in earlier seasons, tying its mythology together as the show moved away from the monster-of-the-week approach, and expanding its universe. However, this reference may not have been made had it not been for Clyde's line originally being misinterpreted.

Found an error? Send it info@screenrant.com so it can be corrected.

Chris Carter's Reddit AMA thread sees a fan ask him if Scully really is immortal, getting the reply, "Yes, it said so right in the show," but Darin Morgan has repeatedly explained that he had meant this line as a way for Clyde to be kind to Scully, knowing how she died, yet denying that she would. He told TVLine, "that was not my intention. That was something fans went crazy with — and they're free to go as crazy as they want." Immortal Scully or not, "Clyde Bruckman's Final Repose" remains one of The X-Files' must-watch episodes.

The Best Ongoing Science Fiction Franchise Is A Secret To 99% Of Fans

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Science fiction comics have no shortage of sprawling universes, but one of the most ambitious currently being built has taken an unusual route to reach readers. Three Worlds / Three Moons, or 3W/3M, is a shared science-fiction setting created by Jonathan Hickman, Mike del Mundo, Mike Huddleston, and Nick Spencer. It began online in 2021, inviting readers to follow the worldbuilding process as it happened rather than simply receiving a finished comic.

That approach has helped 3W/3M grow into something bigger than a conventional comic-book series. Its creators have developed thousands of pages of stories and lore, experimented with digital reading platforms, and built a community around the universe. Dark Horse's 2026 publishing partnership is bringing the project to a much wider comic-shop and bookstore audience, but the printed books are only one part of its appeal.

Hickman introduced the project on August 9, 2021, describing it as a "concept universe" rather than a single storyline. Individual characters, locations, histories, and comics would contribute to a larger whole. Its foundational conflict moves through recurring eras in which science and magic rise and fall, giving the setting room to support stories across vastly different periods.

The premise is deliberately wider than a single space-faring adventure. The worldbuilding ranges across Akva, Therra, and Heir, alongside three moons, with societies, belief systems, economies, and political structures that give each corner of the universe its own identity. The creators wanted those details established before individual plots needed them, so the world could feel as if it existed beyond any protagonist.

The original creative group brought together Hickman with artists del Mundo and Huddleston, whose visual and design work helped shape the setting from the ground up. Nick Spencer later joined the core team, while other writers and artists contributed individual systems and stories. The project was conceived as a place where different creative voices could build connected material, not simply as one writer's long-running serial.

That approach also explains why the series can feel different from a traditional monthly comic. Hickman wanted stories of varying lengths and formats, with releases appearing when the work was ready rather than being forced into a standard issue count. He was also interested in restoring surprise to comics publishing, letting readers encounter new stories and artwork without every release being mapped out through conventional previews.

Jonathan Hickman and Mike del Mundo's 64-page graphic novel

Jonathan Hickman's introduction to the universe

Further exploration of the setting's lore

Geography and locations across the three worlds and three moons

Historical events, stories, and additional lore

The audience was part of the plan from the beginning. In his launch essay, Hickman said the team wanted readers to have a front-row seat during the creation process, with meaningful input at certain stages. That did not mean turning every plot point into a vote or handing over authorship. Instead, the creators made the development of characters, environments, lore, and creative decisions unusually visible.

That transparency became a key part of 3W/3M's identity. Through online posts, the team shared comics alongside process material and conversations about how the universe was designed. Readers could see how visual ideas connected to cultural details, why a place appeared where it did, or how a society's rules influenced the stories set inside it. Worldbuilding became part of the entertainment, not disposable bonus material.

The move into wider print distribution began with Dark Horse's announcement in February 2026. The publisher brought the universe to the direct market with Foundations, a 32-page, magazine-sized one-shot released on July 8 for $6.99. Written by Hickman and Spencer, with art by del Mundo and Huddleston, it follows astronaut and explorer Tajo Vallar on a doomed expedition to a dormant moon, where he uncovers mysteries that reignite an ancient conflict. Foundations builds on earlier digital material, including Fable and Ruin, which introduced Tajo, but gives new readers a clear entry point that does not require navigating years of online posts.

Dark Horse followed that launch with three oversized hardcover sourcebooks announced on July 17, 2026: [SYSTEMS], [MAPS], and [HISTORIES]. Each is planned as a 200-page, 8.5-by-11-inch volume combining comics with reference material and lore. The first, [SYSTEMS], is scheduled for February 2, 2027, at $39.99. Its twelve stories and data pages examine Therran birth assessments, royal duels on Heir, pirate searches for the treasure Khor, and the lore surrounding the gods.

Astarion, Karlach, and other fan-favorite heroes are officially returning in 2027 with new adventures that expand the world of Baldur’s Gate 3.

[MAPS], due April 6, 2027, functions as an atlas, charting locations from Akva's Khor-rich waters to Therra's labyrinths and Heir's rougher districts. It also includes twelve stories, with contributors such as James Stokoe and Juni Ba. [HISTORIES] follows on June 1, 2027, collecting six comics with lore and data that establish the worlds' timeline. Together, the three books offer newcomers a structured introduction to the setting while giving longtime readers more context for its cultures, geography, and past.

Visa Adds Monad to Stablecoin Settlement Options as Institutions Seek More Rails

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Visa’s growing list of blockchain settlement options reportedly has a new entrant: Monad. According to a November 10 report citing the Monad Foundation, participating Visa issuers and acquirers can now use the network for stablecoin settlement. That is a back-office financial infrastructure development, rather than a new consumer-facing Visa card that automatically makes every merchant accept cryptocurrency.

TL;DR: Monad has been added to Visa’s stablecoin settlement options for participating institutional clients, according to a statement from the Monad Foundation reported on November 10. A privacy capability is planned, not yet delivered. The important test will be actual institutional usage and the operational conditions attached to settlement.

When a cardholder pays at a shop, the retail transaction and the subsequent movement of funds between financial institutions are separate processes. A settlement service can use digital dollars on a blockchain to transfer value between participating institutions without requiring consumers to interact with a crypto wallet. That is the layer Visa has been expanding.

Visa’s October 15 update on stablecoin-powered money movement showed that parts of its broader digital-dollar strategy were moving from pilots toward live commercial deployment. Monad’s reported addition extends the selection of networks available to eligible partners; it should not be confused with a universal launch in every market or for every Visa transaction.

For an issuer or acquirer, more than one usable settlement rail can provide choices around transaction processing, cost, operational resilience and supported assets. But multiple chains also bring added technical and compliance responsibilities. Connecting a network is the beginning of integration, not proof that it will process meaningful volumes immediately.

Monad is designed for high-throughput smart-contract execution. The latest announcement also points to a proposed privacy feature intended to shield institutional settlement details from general public view while allowing access for authorized reviewers, including auditors and regulators. That capability is described as coming in future months, so it should not be reported as an existing production guarantee.

The distinction is important. Institutions often need to protect commercially sensitive flows, yet they must also meet audit, recordkeeping and sanctions obligations. Privacy without permitted oversight would not meet all of those needs. Future deployment documentation should explain how access controls work and who can inspect what information.

NewsBTC previously examined how Visa’s treasury engine targets institutional settlement. Separately, Reap’s Monad-linked card infrastructure plan illustrates a different use of the same chain: backing consumer card activity, not necessarily settling balances between institutions.

A blockchain being listed among supported options can improve its credibility with enterprise developers, but that does not establish a direct relationship between network adoption and the market price of its native token. Fees, throughput, partner concentration and the way transaction costs are paid all influence the economics.

What matters next is confirmation of which issuers and acquirers are live, which stablecoins and regions are supported, how settlement is reconciled when something fails, and what measurable transaction volume arrives. Those details would turn a promising infrastructure announcement into evidence of commercial activity. Until then, this is a meaningful new option—not an automatic overhaul of how Visa payments work everywhere.

These Xiaomi phones are no longer getting support with the latest HyperOS 4 update

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Xiaomi's new software has started rolling out in China, but older phones are being left behind.

Owners of a Xiaomi 17 in China are now receiving the latest HyperOS 4 update, which gives Xiaomi's software a new see-through look that's drawn plenty of comparisons to Apple's Liquid Glass. It began rolling out to the company's newest phones and tablets on October 15, 2026, but owners of the Xiaomi 12T and Redmi Note 12 are missing out. The update isn't coming to those models, which are already at, or are about to reach, the end of software support.

According to Xiaomi's official rollout schedule, the first batch of HyperOS 4 updates covers the Xiaomi 17 series, Redmi K100 and K90 series and the Xiaomi Pad 8, with more models following in waves through January 2027. Unfortunately, phones that are reaching the end of support this year, including the Xiaomi 12 and 12T, aren't on that schedule. Firmware trackers have also spotted a handful of cheaper models that, while still within their support windows, haven't been tested with HyperOS 4 at all. Xiaomi hasn't confirmed whether they'll miss out, though, and the rollout is only in China for now.

Xiaomi's schedule for its China firmware rollout lists 57 phones and 15 tablets in total. Following the first batch in mid-October 2026, the Xiaomi 17 Max, 17T and 17T Pro, Redmi K90 Max and Redmi Turbo 5 series were due by October 31, followed by the Xiaomi 15 series, MIX Flip 2, Civi 5 Pro and Redmi K80 series by November 15. The Redmi Note 17 and Note 15 lines come next, by November 30. December's batch is the biggest, taking in the Xiaomi 14 and 13 series, MIX Fold 3 and 4, Redmi K70 series and Redmi Note 14 Pro and 14 5G, while January 2027 is reserved for TVs and wearables.

A global beta opened in September 2026 for the Xiaomi 17, 17 Ultra and the Leica Leitzphone, followed by the 17T, 17T Pro, 15 and 15 Ultra, but Xiaomi hasn't yet provided a date for the global release. Beebom reported on October 11 that Xiaomi still hasn't confirmed a timeline for India or any other market, and Poco phones, which aren't sold in China, don't appear on the schedule at all.

Xiaomi publishes end-of-support dates on its security center site. Gizmochina has counted 19 devices due to drop off in 2026. The Xiaomi 12 and 12 Pro, Redmi Note 12 5G, Redmi 12C and Redmi A2 and A2+ lost support in March, and the Poco F5 and F5 Pro in May. The Xiaomi 12T and 12T Pro reached the end of the line in October, followed by the Redmi 13C and Poco C65 in November. Once a model winds up on that list, it stops receiving OS upgrades and security patches entirely, so it's worth knowing how to check if your Android phone is still getting security updates. Xiaomi's promises have gotten longer since, with the Xiaomi 15 getting four OS updates and six years of security patches, but that's still short of the seven years Samsung and Google now offer on their flagships.

Ximitime, which tracks Xiaomi's firmware, said there were no signs of HyperOS 4 test builds for any phone capped at Android 16. It named six models that hadn't entered testing: the Redmi Note 13 Pro 4G, Redmi Note 14S, Redmi A5 4G, Poco C71, Poco M6 Pro 4G and Poco X6 5G. Xiaomi hasn't confirmed that these phones are excluded, and some still have years of security updates left. If they are included, they'll stay on HyperOS 3.

Interestingly, the Xiaomi 13 series is on December's list, even though Xiaomi promised it three major Android updates, ending at Android 16, back in 2023. However, Xiaomi hasn't said whether its 13 series is getting Android 17 or a version of HyperOS 4 built on Android 16. With four years of security patches promised from its early 2023 global launch, the Xiaomi 13 is also now one of many popular smartphones in their last year of software support.

Xiaomi calls the new design Soft Light Glass, a translucent material that it says adjusts its transparency based on the colors and content behind it. The lock screen's clock can now be resized, moved and restyled, much like the iPhones running iOS 26. Per Xiaomi, the full effect will only run on phones sporting a Snapdragon 8 Elite, Dimensity 9500 or Xiaomi's own XRING O1 chip or newer; older phones on the list get a simplified version.

Xiaomi also claims that HyperOS 4 leaves 27.2 percent more memory free after eight hours of use and opens apps 17.5 percent faster after long sessions. Meanwhile, its voice assistant, Super XiaoAI 2.0, now runs on Xiaomi's own MiMo AI model and adds an Expert Mode that can carry out multi-step tasks across apps, though most of the AI features are limited to China for now. HyperOS 4 is reportedly based on Android 17, but Xiaomi's newest phones already got Android 17 in July 2026 through an interim release called HyperOS 3.3.

Embraer (EMBJ) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Eve's transition flights advance as Embraer's record backlog keeps growing.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Embraer S.A. (EMBJ +7.05%) reported record revenue for the third quarter and reached an all-time high backlog of $35.7 billion. Management raised full-year guidance for revenue and free cash flow based on performance across all four business segments. The company is currently executing production leveling initiatives to balance delivery volumes throughout the year and is investing in new technologies, including the Eve electric vertical takeoff and landing aircraft.

Guilherme Paiva: Good morning, ladies and gentlemen, and thanks for standing by. My name is Gui Paiva, and I'm the Head of Investor Relations, M&A and Venture Capital for Embraer. Welcome to Embraer's Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. As a reminder, this conference is being recorded. This conference call will be conducted in English, but please let me say a short announcement for Portuguese speakers. [Foreign Language]. The agenda includes the presentation of third quarter 2026 results, followed by an investor only Q&A session and subsequently, a session exclusively for the press.
