Museum Organizations Call Bipartisan Infrastruture Bill a ‘Sweeping Assault’ on Historic and Sacred Places

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Some historic preservationists are alarmed at the potential effects of a bipartisan bill whose sponsors say it aims to cut red tape for infrastructure and energy production projects.

On Monday, the US Senate advanced the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026 (S.5653), introduced by Senator Shelley Moore Capito, a West Virginia Republican, by a vote of 73–25. The bill is cosponsored by Senators Mike Lee, a Utah Republican; Sheldon Whitehouse, a Rhode Island Democrat; and Martin Heinrich, a New Mexico Democrat. (The current session of the Republican-controlled Senate runs through December 18; new senators will be sworn in on January 3.) Related Articles American Alliance of Museums Makes Case for Policy Proposals Following Democrats' Landslide Election Win Nearly Everyone Is Concerned About How Museums Use AI in Exhibitions, Survey Says

Crucially, the act would narrow the scope of the National Historic Preservation Act (NHPA), in part by limiting which types of projects trigger review.

Speaking on the Senate floor Monday, Capito said that “for far too long, critical investments in our nation’s infrastructure, energy production, and economy have been forced to sit on the sidelines due to endless red tape, duplicative reviews, and regulatory uncertainty.” Data from the National Association of Manufacturers, she said, “shows that permitting delays are costing America’s manufacturers $12.1 billion every year, including $87 million in additional costs in West Virginia alone. According to new data from McKinsey, $1.8 trillion of investments are locked up in the federal permitting system right now, including $22.3 billion of sidelined investments in my home state of West Virginia.”

According to the Art Newspaper, about 800 protesters gathered on the West Lawn of the US Capitol, among them leaders from the Association of African American Museums, the DC Preservation League, the American Institute of Architects, and the Archaeological Institute of America. Representative Melanie Stansbury, a New Mexico Democrat, called the act a “dirty deal.” Critics cited by the paper noted that the senators introduced the 500-page bill just 48 hours before the Senate vote was scheduled.

S.5653 targets Section 106 of the NHPA, which requires federal agencies to consider the impact of federally funded building projects and requires those receiving federal funds or permits to consult with tribal nations. According to the Art Newspaper, the new bill would make consultation with tribal nations optional and remove the requirement that federal agencies notify and consult with the public and tribal nations about potential harm to historic sites. It would also narrow the definition of what constitutes a historic site.

On November 6, the 106 Strong Coalition, which includes more than 1,200 organizations, tribal nations, and advocates, sent a letter to Congress calling for the defeat of S.5653. Signatories include the American Alliance of Museums, numerous state museum associations, and many individual museums. The letter calls the bill “a sweeping assault on the protections Congress enacted sixty years ago to ensure that federal agencies consider the consequences of their decisions for America’s historic and sacred places.”

Jennifer Lawrence's R-Rated Spy Movie Is One Of HBO Max's Biggest Streaming Hits

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

An R-rated spy thriller starring Jennifer Lawrence is finding new life on HBO Max. The Oscar-winner's 2018 movie "Red Sparrow" has become one of the streamer's biggest hit movies, nearly a decade after its original release.

Directed by Francis Lawrence, who had previously worked with Jennifer Lawrence on "The Hunger Games" movies, "Red Sparrow" is currently the eighth-biggest movie on HBO Max right now, per FlixPatrol. It sits just above "The Wolverine" and just below "The First Purge." Olivia Wilde's comedy "The Invite" is still HBO Max's biggest streaming hit, but the fact that an eight-year-old thriller is right up there with the streamer's current biggest movies is meaningful.

"Red Sparrow" sees Lawrence play a deadly Russian spy. The film centers on Dominika (Lawrence), a former ballerina forced to enter Sparrow School, a government program that thrusts her into a treacherous espionage game. She emerges as a lethal agent, but is trapped in a world she desperately wants to escape. Joel Edgerton ("Warrior"), Matthias Schoenaerts ("Rust and Bone"), Charlotte Rampling ("Broadchurch"), Mary-Louise Parker ("Weeds"), and Ciarán Hinds ("Rome") also star.

The movie is based on the novel of the same name by Jason Matthews. Before it turned into a Lawrence and Lawrence "Hunger Games" reunion, "The Girl With the Dragon Tattoo" director David Fincher and Rooney Mara were poised to reunite for "Red Sparrow." Darren Aronofsky ("Black Swan") was also set to direct at one point. But after much back and forth with a revolving door of A-list talent coming and going, it became a Lawrence starring vehicle. The results were viewed as underwhelming at the time.

Critics weren't terribly kind to the thriller upon its initial release. It currently holds a not-so-great 45% approval rating on Rotten Tomatoes. /Film called "Red Sparrow" an "overlong waste of Jennifer Lawrence's talent" in our review from 2018. It also didn't resonate as well as Fox had hoped it would at the box office at the time.

Against a relatively steep $69 million budget, the movie pulled in $151 million worldwide. It was by no means a hit, especially relative to expectations. Keep in mind that Francis Lawrence and Jennifer Lawrence had a great track record together. "The Hunger Games: Mockingjay — Part 2" ruled the box office just a few years earlier, capping off a multi-billion-dollar run for that franchise. Not to mention that Lawrence was just a handful of years removed from her Oscar win for "Silver Linings Playbook."

Maybe it's because Lawrence remains a big star, with viewers seeking out stuff they haven't seen of hers before. Maybe it's because this sort of action/thriller is the perfect kind of "watch it on the couch" movie. Either way, what was once a disappointment is finding new life all these years later.

You can also grab "Red Sparrow" on 4K, Blu-ray, or DVD from Amazon.

Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce Get Neighbor’s Support for 8-Foot Privacy Wall at $5.35 Million Ohio Estate

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Taylor Swift and Travis Kelce’s plan to build an 8-foot privacy wall at their $5.35 million Ohio estate is headed back before a local review board, and at least one neighbor says the proposal has more support than the backlash suggests.

The Kansas City Chiefs tight end, 36, snapped up the eight-bedroom lakefront mansion in March for $5.35 million.

Since then, Kelce and Swift have reportedly taken steps to keep prying eyes away from the historical residence, including introducing plans to construct an 8-foot wall around the property—which were rejected in September.

Speaking anonymously to Page Six ahead of the board’s next meeting, the neighbor pushed back on the idea that the proposal had angered residents.

“I think they’re excited. I think it’s great. I don’t think people are angry,” the neighbor told the outlet.

The Bratenahl Architectural Review Board previously considered the proposal on Aug. 11 and Sept. 8 and is scheduled to take it up again on Nov. 10.

The proposed wall appears to conflict with local regulations, which state that solid fences and walls—as well as fences with less than 75% openness—cannot exceed 30 inches in height.

According to TMZ, a new proposal has since been submitted, although it remains unclear what changes have been made to the original plans.

The setback came after the couple implemented other stringent security measures at the property.

Page Six previously reported that the residence is equipped with a sophisticated security system capable of issuing verbal warnings to anyone who ventures too close.

“A neighbor approached the house and got a voice warning about not passing onto the property after getting too close to the house,” a nearby resident told the outlet.

Built in 1895, the residence spans more than 21,000 square feet and sits on approximately 3.5 acres along Lake Erie.

The mansion boasts eight bedrooms and 11 bathrooms, as well as multiple fireplaces, a home theater, game room, wine cellar, fitness center, sauna, and heated pool overlooking the lake.

The estate is located near Cleveland and is also close to a private airport, according to the outlet—offering the star athlete a convenient launching point for trips with his wife, 36.

Kelce purchased the dwelling from Figurati Construction Group, which is owned by Gary Pellegrino. The company acquired the property for $1.95 million in 2023.

The home was subsequently listed for $6.99 million in 2024 before its price was reduced to $5.99 million in May 2025. Kelce ultimately secured the estate for $5.35 million.

Located in Bratenahl, OH, the property sits just a few miles from Cleveland Heights, where Kelce grew up alongside his older brother, Jason Kelce.

The tiny village is nestled along the southern shore of Lake Erie and offers a mix of multimillion-dollar estates and more modest residences. Homes currently on the market in the area range in price from roughly $250,000 to $2.4 million, according to Realtor.com® data.

The transaction was represented by David Ayers and Nick Zawitz of The Agency Cleveland.

“We’re thrilled for the new owners and so excited for Cleveland,” Ayers and Zawitz said in a statement.

News of their purchase came just one day after it was confirmed that Kelce and Swift added a fluffy white dog to their family.

He introduced the couple's adorable white pup to the world while starring in Tommy Hilfiger's fall 2026 ad campaign.

The sweet cameo confirmed months of speculation that Kelce and Swift had quietly expanded their family with a Samoyed in 2025.

Just days after Kelce and Swift wed, it was revealed that the pup had become such a key part of their lives that it even played a role in their wedding celebrations.

The "Lover" singer is known to have a very soft spot for her own pets: three cats, named Meredith Grey, Olivia Benson, and Benjamin Button.

It's not yet known how the felines have responded to the family's newest member.

However, the dog playing such a key role in Swift and Kelce's wedding ceremony indicates what a big part of their lives it has become—with a framed photo of the pup taking center stage in one of the few glimpses of the venue leaked since the wedding.

A spokesperson for the couple did later confirm reports that the ceremony was officiated by Adam Sandler, while also sharing details about the couple's wedding-day looks.

According to the official release, both the bride and groom sported Christian Dior Haute Couture, Christian Louboutin shoes, and Cartier jewelry—and neither had a bridal party. Instead, they relied on their siblings to be by their side during the ceremony.

“Taylor and Travis did not have bridesmaids or groomsmen. Austin Swift served as Taylor’s Man of Honor, Jason Kelce was Travis’ Best Man," the statement read.

According to People, the singer walked down the aisle to an acoustic version of her hit "Love Story."

The A-list couple joined a long line of celebrities who have wed in New York City, where Swift has owned property for more than a decade.

Kelsi Karruli is a senior celebrity reporter who joined Realtor.com in 2025. She writes about trends encompassing real estate and celebrity properties. She previously wrote for Daily Mail, True Urban Culture, and JMedia Corporation, where she specialized in covering celebrities, relationships, psychology, and gender issues. She has a bachelor's degree in journalism from Pace University, where she received the James Rose Award for excellence in journalism. When she isn’t updating readers on A-listers' moves, she is spending time with her cat, Mary, and cheering on the Albanian soccer team.

Brokies, This Met Gala Is for You

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

T-shirts revolutionize the Costume Institute’s programming, the sociability of MoMA’s Brancusi exhibit, and the curators of the 2028 Whitney Biennial announced.

A saturated blue sky and cascades of complementary golden-yellow foliage laid the foundation for a vibrant, picturesque Diwali weekend in the suburbs. It wasn’t the most festive Diwali for me — instead of lighting diyas and playing with sparklers, I spent a majority of the weekend hand-feeding a friendly yard squirrel while wearing some bullshit pajamas extracted from the depths of my childhood closet.

Think I’m slumming it? Well, think again! Because those dilapidated pajamas are almost certainly what inspired the theme for next year’s Met Gala and related Costume Institute exhibit: extraOrdinary: Fashion and the Poetics of the Everyday, a program “devoted to the undersung beauty and allure of quotidian garments.” (That’s code for jeans.)

(And t-shirts. And raincoats. Maybe pajamas, but also maybe not.)

It’s an exasperated, last-minute pivot after controversial British fashion designer John Galliano, who was central to the Costume Institute’s original program for 2027, withdrew from participating entirely this August in response to the public backlash toward his racist behavior in the past. Between “elevating” daily wear into runway fashion and exploring how “humble, even ‘disposable’ materials” like paper and aluminum can become wearable, this enormous exercise in putting a positive spin on gentrifying economic recession is at odds with NYC residents who are buckling from profound unaffordability as the city speedruns its transformation into a private equity playground.

I guess I can’t really say which is worse — the current standard of celebrities in bespoke, extravagant garments from elite fashion houses hanging off their GLP-1 frames, or the notion that a Met Gala dress code calling for aluminum foil pants, a shrunken tee, and maybe a jacket made from hole-punched plastic circles is what’ll push people to consider everyday wear in a different light.

However, before we get too pressed, we have to acknowledge that this is a big moment for the Bad Bunny x Zara collection! On the plus side, it might make it easier for some ~lowly civs~ to sneak in and eat all the cheese cubes. Dress for the job you want, right?

Speaking of jobs — Whitney Museum curator Meg Onli returns as a co-curator for the 2028 Whitney Biennial, sharing the stage with director and dancer Zack Winokur, who co-founded the interdisciplinary American Modern Opera Company in 2017. I found the 2026 Biennial to be a textbook case of the whole being less than the sum of its parts. Let’s see how Winokur’s hand in artistic performance direction manifests in this curatorial behemoth; I’m sure he’s used to working with a lot of moving parts.

Back to the here and now, I’m long overdue for multiple museum visits at the peak of the season. The days keep slipping through my fingers before I’ve had a chance to visit the Krasner Pollock show at The Met or the Architects of Liberation at the MoMA. Mayor Mamdani actually paid a visit to the MoMA show before I did, and he’s significantly busier than I am, so there’s really no excuse!

In another nudge for me to make the journey uptown, MoMA recently opened its Brancusi exhibition. With a replica of Brancusi’s studio and a selection of rare media documenting his work environment and the social atmosphere he cultivated between his sculptures and his guests alike, the exhibition spotlights the artist’s labor-intensive process as an art form in itself.

Critic Aruna D’Souza noted that the “sculptures seem to be maintaining COVID-era social distancing protocols,” and chalked that awkward dynamic up to the MoMA’s renovated gallery layout, recalling the previously approachable setup’s capacity to “allow for art objects to talk to each other.”

On Sunday, I bore witness to a conversation that made me think about Brancusi’s glinting buffed bronzes and smooth marbles. The chatter between a yellowing tree branch, an overturned recycling bin, and the white picket fence behind them might have been too maximalist compared to the Romanian sculptor’s sleek, pared-down modernism. Nevertheless, I pulled over to eavesdrop.

Among the historic wave of triumphs for Democrats in yesterday’s midterms, New York State Assemblywoman Claire Valdez, an artist, former cultural worker, and union organizer, was elected to represent New York’s 7th Congressional District in the United States House of Representatives. | Anna Lee

Having co-curated the 2024 Biennial, Onli returns to the next edition with Winokur, who founded the American Modern Opera Company. | Anna Lee

The artist’s work is glorious and resonant, but the Museum of Modern Art has a space problem, or an elegance problem, or a crowd mitigation problem. | Aruna D’Souza

A little more legibility would’ve benefited Alison Nguyen’s fragmented short film about the famed NYC artists’ housing complex. | Rhea Nayyar

This residency at the U-M Stamps School, in partnership with the U-M Museum of Art (UMMA), offers artists a $20,000 honorarium, housing, studio space, and a culminating exhibition in UMMA’s prominent Stenn Gallery.

The Bayeux tapestry, a new biography of Edward Hopper, The Met's exhibition catalogue on Krasner and Pollock, what architecture is, and more

Funding for tuition, healthcare, and stipends is available. Apply to the University of Maryland, Baltimore County program by Feb 1 to be considered for financial support.

Generously funded, renowned faculty and visiting artists, spacious studios, and an exhibition at the Manetti Shrem Museum of Art.

Ring cameras go mobile as Amazon debuts AI-powered security drone and robot

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Amazon’s roaming Ring Robotics devices will debut in late 2026 and early 2027.

Amazon is betting that many Ring customers will also want an AI-powered security drone and robot patrolling their homes or offices. But the company’s upcoming Ring Robotic products are also certain to intensify privacy concerns previously raised around a prior Amazon attempt to popularize a flying security camera.

The Ring Rover Cam and Ring Drone Cam are expected to reach customers starting at the end of 2026 and in early 2027, respectively, according to an Amazon announcement posted on November 10. Amazon describes the devices as relying on an AI Pilot system that uses each device’s cameras along with “specialized vision models, on-device sensors and cloud processing” to plan patrol routes while autonomously navigating around obstacles such as furniture.

Both devices also use that AI Pilot system to digitally map the floor plan of the customer’s home or business. This setup process occurs after the customer carries the Ring drone through their indoor spaces or allows the Ring Rover to do some autonomous exploration.

The AI Pilot can supposedly use that guided- or self-tour information to identify specific rooms and suggest patrol points near side doors, windows, or even sensitive locations such as a cash register in the case of a business.

However, flying and roving cameras with AI-enabled features could introduce new privacy risks for homes and businesses, on top of existing concerns involving fixed Ring cameras installed in room corners or as doorbell cameras.

Amazon’s Ring Drone appears to be a direct successor to the company’s Ring Always Home Cam that was only temporarily available for purchase by invitation as a “Day 1 Edition” product in September 2021—even having the same starting price of $250.

But the Ring Drone still requires authorization from the Federal Communications Commission before going on sale. Assuming that comes through, Amazon aims to make the drone available on January 27, 2027, with customers able to register for email updates through the product page.

Onboard camera capability includes a 148-degree field of view and 360-degree rotation with a 2K video resolution. Two LED lights also activate in low-light conditions to help illuminate the scene for the drone video camera.

However, the drone is limited to just 8 minutes of flight time when operating on a “new, fully-charged battery under controlled conditions” according to the product description. It comes with a docking station for charging.

Another caveat in the product description is that the Ring Drone Cam may experience degraded performance when navigating in environments with reflective surfaces such as “polished marble or wet tile,” blank matte walls without any features, or transparent surfaces like frameless glass and mirrors.

Still, Amazon touts the drone as providing an “autonomous way to check on your home that has never existed before,” whether the customer is away or at home. The Ring Drone can be controlled directly from the Alexa Home Map interactive map that shows all of a customer’s Amazon-linked devices.

Meanwhile, Amazon has pitched the Ring Rover Cam priced at $1,000 as “an autonomous security robot designed for business.” This product is already available for preorder for those so inclined and scheduled to start shipping to customers on December 16, 2026.

The company described the Ring Rover as being inspired by customer feedback involving another invite-only beta product, the Amazon Astro robot designed for home monitoring.

The Ring Rover can livestream and record in up to 4K video resolution when patrolling or while doing a 360-degree scan at a designated location. Videos triggered by doorbell rings, motion events and “Live View” events are stored in the cloud for sharing and replaying for up to 180 days.

Like the Ring Drone, the Ring Rover can automatically respond to alerts from connected Ring devices by following its preprogrammed patrol points. But the wheeled robot is only designed to operate within a single-floor, indoor environment because it lacks stair-climbing capability.

Amazon’s product description also warns that the Rover is not compatible with “curved edges, sunken areas, flooring transitions greater than 1.375 inches (3.5 centimeters), and black glossy floors.”

To help compensate for its ground-based view, the Ring Rover comes with an automatically adjustable mast to peer over counters, shelving and other obstacles. It can supposedly cover up to 5,000 square feet (465 square meters), according to Amazon’s product description.

The Ring Rover also comes with additional security-related features such as a siren and light tower to loudly announce its presence, along with a microphone for two-way audio communication if the customer wants to address someone near the robot.

Amazon touts the new Ring Robotics products as offering “privacy and security without compromise,” with features such as LED indicators and screen notifications informing customers when video and audio recordings are active. But Ring, as an Amazon subsidiary, has been previously implicated in multiple privacy and security scandals.

Ring was previously charged in a 2023 Federal Trade Commission investigation with allowing employees and contractors to watch Ring video recordings of customers’ private spaces. The FTC also described Ring as having lackluster safeguards that allowed hackers to access 55,000 US customers’ accounts.

Videos taken by the new Ring Drone and Ring Rover will be protected with Ring’s Throw Away the Key Encryption that was introduced in September 2026, according to Amazon’s announcement. The digital rights nonprofit Electronic Frontier Foundation (EFF) has described this as an improvement over Ring’s former default settings, but still well short of end-to-end encryption.

The EFF has also called out what it describes as Ring founder Jamie Siminoff’s “surveillance-first-privacy-last” approach to Ring devices, including Ring reintroducing features that allow law enforcement to request footage or live-stream access from Ring users’ home security devices.

Amazon’s previous attempt to launch its Ring Always Home Cam drone also led to expanded privacy concerns. Ryan Calo, a law and technology researcher at the University of Washington, previously described such a flying security camera as providing new ways to surveil others indoors, along with giving companies much greater access to potentially sensitive or identifying details of homes and businesses.

There is currently no mention of the new Ring Robotics products implementing facial recognition or similar AI-powered technologies for identifying people. But starting in December 2025, Ring has allowed owners of fixed Ring doorbell cameras to turn on a “Familiar Faces” feature that uses facial recognition technology on visitors—and that is already the subject of a class action lawsuit.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Inside OM Digital Solutions’ Okaya Manufacturing Site

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Inside OM Digital Solutions’ Okaya Manufacturing Site

I know how to take pictures, but ask me how the lens on my camera is made and I have a lot less to say. A visit to the Okaya Manufacturing Site in Japan, followed by some research afterward, gave me a lesson in a subject I’d never understood particularly well. I wanted to understand how the glass inside a lens is crafted, how the tools used in that process are prepared, and how people learn to do that work.

The facility was OM Digital Solutions’ Okaya Manufacturing Site in Nagano Prefecture, Japan. This was an overwhelming experience from a learning standpoint, so there was quite a bit that went over my head as we moved from station to station. Luckily, follow-up discussions with OM SYSTEM and research helped me fill in some of the gaps.

Okaya is the first optical lens manufacturing site in Japan for OM Digital Solutions, the company behind OM SYSTEM. It opened in 2025 and does two jobs. It produces lenses that are made in small quantities but come in many different types, and it works as a pilot factory, a place where the manufacturing processes for technically demanding new lenses are developed and proven before they’re used at scale.

The site works closely with the optical design team at the company’s headquarters in Hachioji, Tokyo. Designing a lens and figuring out how to build it are two different jobs that call for different expertise. Okaya’s engineers handle the second one, turning a design into a production process that actually works.

Mass production happens at the company’s factory in Vietnam. Before a production line for a new lens launches there, Okaya verifies the production technology, the manufacturing processes, and the work methods it will use. Working those out in Japan first is meant to make production in Vietnam more efficient and keep the quality consistent.

The place to begin is with an individual lens element, one of the many pieces of glass inside a camera lens. OM SYSTEM’s M.Zuiko Digital ED 40-150mm F2.8 PRO, for example, contains 16 of these elements. Before the lens can be assembled, each of these pieces of glass needs the correct curve and a smooth surface.

A piece of glass can be clear and look correct but still bend light the wrong way. A lens bends light to bring an image into focus on the camera’s sensor. Light from each point in the scene needs to come together at a matching point on the sensor. The curves of the glass help direct it there. If a curve is wrong, that light can spread around its intended point instead, making a small detail look blurry.

The sequence OM SYSTEM showed us at the Okaya Manufacturing Site starts with grinding, which gradually wears down the glass to form the basic curve. Finer grinding makes smaller adjustments to the glass. Polishing then wears away more material to give the surface the smoothness and accuracy it needs.

I got a small taste of this myself. During a workshop at Okaya, I tried lens polishing, including mounting the lens and taking it off again afterward. It looked simple. Then a slight mistake in my setup chipped the lens. That was enough to show me how delicate the process is and how much care and precision it takes.

Glass can look clear and smooth to the naked eye and still have flaws that affect the photograph. Tiny scratches, bumps, and dips, or a surface that strays slightly from its intended shape, can scatter light in directions it shouldn’t go. That can cause flare, the haze or bright spots stray light leaves in a picture, and lower the contrast, so the image looks flatter.

If the surface doesn’t match its designed shape, light won’t bend and come together the way the designers intended. So polishing has two jobs: leave the surface free of scratches, and reproduce the designed shape to within nanometers, or billionths of a meter. The engineers check the glass with a range of measuring instruments, but they also rely on experience and skill to make delicate adjustments to each lens.

Then they have to do it again on the next piece of glass, and the one after that. Even a slight difference between individual lenses can change how light travels through the finished lens and affect image quality.

Each glass element also needs to sit in the right position inside the lens. Imagine fitting one into a holder. It could fit neatly while the curved faces that bend light sit slightly off-center. That mismatch can send light to the wrong place. A manufacturing step called centering corrects that mismatch before assembly.

Getting the curve right and getting the glass into the right position both matter to the photograph. Even when we focus correctly, errors in the glass’s shape or alignment can leave fine details looking soft.

Even the tools OM Digital Solutions uses at the Okaya Manufacturing Site need preparation before they can work on the glass. These are the parts fitted to the machines that grind and polish the lens elements. The tools the engineers use to work on the glass need smooth, accurately made surfaces, too.

One of these tools has a small, round face that touches the glass. To prepare it, an engineer uses a second tool: a disc on the end of a handle. The engineer holds the disc against the first tool’s face, polishing the two surfaces together.

The accuracy of the tool affects the accuracy of the surface it produces on the glass.

I was surprised to see that they still prepare some of their tools by hand. The precision required helped me appreciate the care the engineers bring to preparing those tools and building the lenses. At Okaya, I saw veteran engineers working alongside younger colleagues to pass on their skills. Both generations were full of excitement and pride as they showed us how they make lenses in their facility.

That exchange between generations is a big part of how the skills developed at the Okaya Manufacturing Site get passed down. OM Digital Solutions says the younger engineers’ questions also encourage the veterans to keep improving their own skills. While watching them work, I could see how explaining a familiar task to someone else can make you think more carefully about the way you do it.

The training follows an apprenticeship model, with coaching taking place during manufacturing work such as polishing. Part of the challenge is passing on knowledge that comes from experience and can be difficult to put into written instructions.

With its younger engineers in mind, the company is also exploring other ways to pass on the skills and judgment that are hard to capture in a written document.

Along with their manufacturing skills, OM Digital Solutions also wants the experienced engineers to pass on their way of thinking about the work.

If I were explaining one of my photographs to another photographer, I’d want to talk about why I chose a particular aperture for depth of field or focused on a certain part of the scene. Understanding those choices could help them make decisions about their own photographs. Knowing why a manufacturing step is done a certain way could help younger engineers decide what to keep and where to make improvements. They can build on that experience to improve both how lenses are made and how those lenses perform.

When a photographer buys a lens, they expect it to meet the same standard as other copies of that model. That depends on being able to make its components accurately, again and again.

At Okaya, engineers test early versions of lens components for new products. They also check new manufacturing methods to see whether they are suitable for producing lenses in larger quantities. Once those methods have been checked, they can be transferred to the factory in Vietnam.

Some of this involves tasks as routine as loading parts into machines and taking them out again. They are automating repetitive jobs like these to help improve production quality and efficiency.

The relationship between the two sites continues long after production begins. If an issue turns up while a mass-production process is being verified at the Vietnam factory, the team at Okaya can reproduce the same issue on purpose. Recreating it gives the engineers a controlled way to analyze what went wrong, find the root cause, and develop a fix. Each round of that back-and-forth turns highly specialized know-how into a process the factory can repeat with the same quality, lens after lens.

Learning about OM Digital Solutions’ Okaya Manufacturing Site helped me understand the work, passion, and dedication that go into their lens making. I had never given the process much thought and, I admit, I assumed it was easier than it actually is. The hand preparation of the tools was a good example. Once I understood how a tool’s accuracy affects the glass it works on, I could see why that preparation needed so much care.

It also showed me how seriously the company takes quality. Nearly every step I learned about, from measuring a polished surface to deliberately recreating a problem from the Vietnam factory, exists to make sure a lens performs the way it was designed to, and that the next copy does too.

Understanding the work gave me more respect for the engineers who showed it to us and for the experience they are passing on to younger colleagues. As a travel photographer, I’ve often taken my gear for granted. I now have a better idea of what goes into making a lens and a much greater appreciation for the lenses that have helped me document the world over the last 15 years.

Cardinals place QB Gardner Minshew and WR Michael Wilson on IR

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Cardinals placed quarterback Gardner Minshew and wide receiver Michael Wilson on injured reserve Tuesday, the team announced.

Minshew tore the UCL in his elbow and will miss the rest of the season. Wilson’s neck injury will end his season.

“Not the ending I had envisioned, but grateful for the journey,” Wilson wrote on social media. “Regardless of what happens, my love for the game will never change! Thank you for all the love and support. The story is still being written.”

Wilson played eight games in 2026, making 51 receptions for 546 yards and two touchdowns.

In two appearances, Minshew completed 38 of 53 passes for 354 yards and three touchdowns, and led the Cardinals to an overtime win in Dallas in Week 8. He is scheduled to be a free agent at the end of the season.

The Cardinals signed veteran quarterback Josh Johnson and rookie quarterback Joey Aguilar to back up rookie Carson Beck. Beck will get his first NFL start on Sunday against the Rams.

Aguilar was on the Jaguars’ practice squad, and Johnson was on the Bengals’ practice squad.

The Cardinals also signed receiver Phillip Dorsett II to the practice squad and released tight end Rivaldo Fairweather and running back Jermar Jefferson from the practice squad.

Three Prada Obsessives Unpack Miuccia’s Defining Decade

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Bazaar’s Lynette Nylander and Camille Freestone join Laia Garcia-Furtado for a roundtable on her new book, Prada in the Nineties

Prada is as much an adjective as it is a noun. My colleagues and I often describe wardrobe twists, like a bejeweled brooch on a Peter Pan collar or an embroidered sock peeking out of a Mary Jane, as “very Prada.” But what is it about these little notes of character that signals “Prada” in our brain?

That’s one of the questions Bazaar’s deputy culture director, Laia Garcia-Furtado, answers in her new book, Prada in the Nineties. (Published by Batsford, it drops today.) Her deep dive into one of the brand’s most pivotal decades reaches far beyond the heroin-chic label we tend to slap on ’90s fashion. From runway reviews to cultural anecdotes, Garcia-Furtado takes us backstage, into the life and personality of Miuccia Prada, the industry’s north star of intellectual fashion.

Mrs. Prada’s arsenal of off-color prints and skirts have inspired generations of women to aspire to an ugly-chic kind of cool; looking at her today, it’s easy to find ourselves lost in her world. But in harkening back to the nascent stages of her career as a designer, Garcia-Furtado reminds us that the throughline of her genius lies in her sense of self.

It’s a story that began with nylon, an unexpected choice for a member of the Prada family, with its legacy of selling bespoke leather goods to royal clientele. Yet Mrs. Prada’s choice to bring nylon totes and duffels to the luxury market was not merely driven by rebellion—it was a manifestation of her own desire for a well-designed bag that embraced utility. And the groundbreaking success of the bags demonstrated that her dreams were shared by other women—a hint of where Prada would take the brand.

Garcia-Furtado understands this lore firsthand. After all, she’s spent a lot of time in Mrs. Prada’s shoes—well, the ones she designed, at least. She began building her own archive of Miuccia merch in college with a pair of woven leather Mary Janes by Prada’s little sister, Miu Miu. A lucky eBay find from the spring 2001 collection, they were marked by interlaced checks of leather with pops of plum purple and canvas buckle straps. She has continued to build her collection ever since.

Writing the book gave Garcia-Furtado carte blanche to dig into the nitty-gritty details that unite women of varying tastes and lifestyles as proud members of Prada’s hive. Below, she gathers with Bazaar’s fellow Prada obsessees, executive digital director Lynette Nylander and senior fashion writer Camille Freestone, to unpack all the things that have placed Mrs. Prada in a league of her own, from the ’90s to now.

Prada in the Ninteties by Laia Garcia-Furtado

Jennifer Jenkins: What inspired you to go on such a deep dive on Miuccia Prada?

Laia Garcia-Furtado: There are very specific images that we go to when we think of Prada in the ’90s, but all of those collections are from the late ’90s. So I thought, "Wait, what about the early '90s?" When I started looking up those collections, I realized that what I was really doing was tracing how Prada went from almost an independent, fledgling designer to an icon in a period of ten years, which is really crazy. I love to go down a research hole; that's actually my favorite thing. It was just great to have an excuse.

Lynette Nylander: One of the things that struck me about going through Laia’s book, and my own personal understanding of Prada in the '90s, is that it was really foundational. A lot of the signatures and markers of what Prada is today were made in that formative time. One of my favorite things about being part of the Prada hive—and I think we all identify as that—is that she has carefully constructed a brand where you can mine from its past. She's very okay with the idea that something is a rehashed idea from a '90s collection in the 2000s, in the 2010s, in the 2020s. Everything is meant to be in dialogue with everything else. That's really refreshing in a world where designers are making very distinct collections and basically almost asking you to do away with whatever you bought last year and buy into what's “now.” She very much respects the past, which is why I think a book on the '90s is brilliant.

Camille Freestone: Also, the designs are so pared back. We think now of much more heavy-handed embellishment when we imagine Prada. Part of it was that it was the '90s and it was just a simpler time. Runways have just gotten more megawatt altogether. But the Prada collections are quite pure. The design is quite simple in the best way, so they let her creative touches shine in such a way that feels very present.

LGF: I mean everything old is new, et cetera. Going back to what you were saying about the simple shapes, even when things are heavily embellished and there's a lot of beading and sequins, it never feels like putting on a bunch of stuff for the sake of putting on a bunch of stuff. Even if it's super over-the-top, you think, “It's exactly what it needs to be.” It feels very real. One of my favorite things I read while I was doing research is a quote where she's saying how in movies and in art, you're allowed to bring in real life and it's allowed to be ugly and regular, but fashion was a place where you weren't allowed to do that, and she wanted to change that. When I read that, everything from those old collections made so much sense.

But even now, I think you can read a collection, even if she and Raf are saying that it's about one thing, and you look at it, you're like, "It's really about something else."

LN: Yeah, and that's always been a part of that duality. And I mean, even in the inception of Miu Miu, she talks about it as this other side of her personality. She's someone who is really in dialogue with her more fantastical, imagined self. And I think what was interesting about going through the '90s collections was that it was fantastical, but again, more real.

But I want to talk about what you learned. They always say hindsight is 20/20, but I don't think we were lucky enough to understand what a unique time the ‘90s were. It was pre-social-media. Celebrity culture had hit this apex, and Miuccia was there as this brand that carried and collected a lot of celebrity in a way that is not how it is now.

LGF: It wasn’t grody! And I think that was especially evident when she launched menswear, and John Malkovich was her model or Willem Dafoe—I mean, now we all love Willem Dafoe, of course, but it was very different from what everyone else was doing.

And another thing that's really interesting is that we have a vision of the ’90s, but the first couple of years of the ‘90s are still the ’80s. It takes years for the prevailing aesthetic that we all remember to rise up. Going back, Prada was a heritage label, but she launched ready-to-wear in the late ‘80s. And it wasn't like when Vuitton launched ready-to-wear, when everybody understood it as a major brand. Prada was by no means in its flop era, but it wasn’t as well known as Vuitton. It was not the same.

So when she started, critics were writing things like “Prada also showed a collection.” There wasn't even a long review. And then she sort of started getting talked about in the same breath as Martin Margiela or Ann Demeulemeester, like, “These people are the plucky new designers.” We think she's so untouchable now and it's like she almost can do no wrong, but you can go back and see how it all started and see her be like, "I hated this collection actually, and I'll never do that again." I mean, my kingdom for any designer that would say that now—to just be that honest.

JJ: You touched a lot on her unapologetic way of expressing herself, even while taking in different cultural references and touchpoints. Was there anything in particular you were most surprised to learn about her?

LGF: My favorite trivia is that she got married on Valentine's Day.

LGF: She's a romantic. I feel like that says so much.

CF: It's amazing. She was in the Communist Party of Italy, and so not of the fashion world at all, but so endlessly fascinated by it.

LGF: It's so funny because it's very much like her parents said, "Okay, you've had your fun. Now, come back home, join the family business.” And she wanted to be an actor—the mime thing! In one interview, she said, "My favorite part was when we had to do really specific body control stuff." And you can totally see her just being so good at that.

LN: Well, I think that she's so good at it because I can't think of a designer, at least in more modern history, that really sort of mines from herself. Have you ever noticed, if you read a part of a review, or you’ve seen those videos of her backstage, it's never like, "I was looking at the art of Georgia O’Keeffe”?

LN: She doesn't even mine it from a place. She isn't even saying, "Oh, I was walking down the piazza in Milan." It's not about that—it's this higher sense of self. I think about the fall/winter 2026 collection, with Bella Hadid and the four rounds of looks. And of course she did that because she's the only person who would do that, because those are the things that ticktock around in the caverns of her mind when we're all fast asleep.

CF: Well, she's oddly practical. She's kind of utilitarian. And I think now I'm just saying a different thing in different words, but you think of a crystal embellishment when you think of Prada, you think of a quirk, but it's all rooted in a really utilitarian approach. I feel like this is clothing, not art.

LGF: It's also because that's how she dresses. I mean, yes, are her high heels too high for us to walk and not get blisters? Yes, but we still wear them. It's fine. But she designs clothes that she wants to wear, and we always look at what she's wearing at the end of the show to see how she's styling.

LN: She's the model that we're looking for at the end of the show.

CF: Then it's funny to put her back in dialogue with a Martin Margiela; they both were subversive in their own ways, but hers was lensed through an idea that these are just clothes. You need to get dressed in them!

LGF: There is something about the two of them. Margiela never wanted to be interviewed, but when you look back at a lot of the profiles of her, there will be editorials where it's designers with their muses, and she's almost always by herself. She's her own muse.

It's just her. It's like her looking in a mirror. It's her and her son, or it's her by herself, but it's such a clear vision. And I mean, I think it also makes a difference because a lot of the male designers need a muse…but for her, it's always her.

CF: I'm also always fascinated by the “designer uniform.” There are so many examples. Phoebe Philo, Michael Kors, Jonathan Anderson: everyone takes their bow, and it's almost like they're the antithesis of their clothes. So obviously, we are always talking about a woman designing for women, but in that particular context of those big houses, she's one of the few who is just dressing just like her collection.

LGF: And it's amazing the few times she's come out wearing pants.

LGF: It's so rare! You're like, "Oh my god, the slim cigarette.”

LN: I have two things I want to say to the idea that she doesn't really have a muse. The muse is herself, but somehow that doesn't come across as arrogant. It's not the opposite. It comes across as, “I just do what I know I’ll respond to and hopefully other people like it.” And I think there's something really earnest about it.

CF: There's also no ideal of the perfect woman, either.

LN: Yes, in fact, I don't think she even really deals or trades in ideals.

I also want to talk about the colors. I mean, if I think about Prada, I just think about this kaleidoscopic universe of color. She can go anywhere. She can put any color combination together. It's kind of genius. But particularly in the '90s… they're all quite muted. You have this line from Robin Givhan in the book, “The greens hovered somewhere between shades of slime and mold. The browns were”—I mean, this is the beauty of the Robin Givhan written line—“the browns were murky, the color of water as it stagnates over a long, steamy summer. The dizzy and crisscross patterns were reminiscent of UHF static or an old-fashioned labyrinth in rusted plumbing.”

LGF: It's funny though because Miuccia Prada hated all of those collections.

LN: I think if there was ever going to be a brand that's going to respect the fact that Robin Givhan ripped her to shreds, it's going to be her. I think she believes in the idea of free thought, and that was Givhan’s thought and put so beautifully.

But I do want to talk about the colors, because I just think it's so specific of how I think about Prada, but also how I think about my own relationship to color. I don't know if you feel like that, but probably in an inadvertent way, she has given me the confidence and the permission to put together these really disparate color combinations.

LGF: I mean, I think that collection that Robin is talking about, from spring ’96 (which is the root cause of my everything; I had a slime green room—I love slime green) is really interesting. Even before she got into the cool color combinations that we know her from, those early '90s collections, even when it was like, “Here's a bunch of beautiful white dresses,” suddenly there would be weird goldenrod silk and a weird orange. The color thing was always there from the beginning. And when you look at the collections throughout the years, you see how she gets a little bit more confident in that and starts mixing it. But that punch of weirdness was present from the beginning.

CF: But somehow, I think, "Why do I like this slime shade? Why do I like this brown? Why do I like this purple?" But all of a sudden, she's made me want purple.

LN: I hate purple in any other context than hers—I need purple.

CF: All of a sudden I want purple. And there's these weird plaids that all of a sudden make me think, "Oh, I need that."

LGF: Also, in the late '90s, she does collections with things embellished with mirrors, and she was like, "Well, everyone is being a hippie and doing this, so I also did it,” but she did it extra crazy. And I love when she is engaging.

Going back to what you said about her being probably one of the few designers that is like, "This review panned me, it's fine,” I feel like she really was engaging with what was being written about her.

One season editors were like, "There are no suits. This is outrageous! Where are all the suits?" So then the next season she opened the collection with a suit and it's very like, "You're welcome. Are you happy now?” I like to see her engage, not that she doesn't do that anymore, but I think in the ’90s, it was a little bit more obvious.

LN: And now she kind of hovers above it all. I want to talk about 1998. Her ascent was complete, you say, in 1998, when she was now at over a half a billion dollars in sales. She landed at 15 in Vogue’s list of the most influential brands. Other women at the top 20 were Donna Karan, Angela Missoni, and Mariuccia Mandelli, and Miuccia is one of the only working designers left. What was happening in 1998, and what collection is that?

LGF: It's the one with the cool red-and-white boots that are space-age. It's all very space-age mod. ‘98 is the time when she went all-in on the millennial aesthetic that everyone was into, when she did her sports thing.

CF: I also think she doesn't deal very much in volume. That's not really her game.

LN: Thank god. I don't know if I'd want a poofy Prada.

LGF: Yeah. But it's so crazy—in a decade you go from reviews that say, "Oh, Prada also showed a collection," to, "Everyone is focused on whatever she does."

LGF: I think she just became more confident in her vision, which was different from everyone else. And I mean, Tom Ford was also doing something at Gucci; there was that vibe shift, and she was part of it, but it was different. From the beginning, she was always the thinking woman's sex-appeal designer, and that just stayed. She was very in tune with the culture, but she wasn't just doing reproductions of what you could see. She was injecting it with her own aesthetic.

You can look at something and say, "Oh, this was her way of doing grunge," but it was not grunge. It was her own thing.

LN: What was that thing? I mean, do you look at broader context and get a sense of the music she listens to or the boys she kissed or the movies she liked?

LGF: Yeah, I think it's very much a movies thing. I feel like you get a sense of her upbringing. The Mary Jane—the shoes she wore as a little girl—were always a thing. She got married in a dress and a coat made by a childrenswear designer. The little socks—you start seeing those reflections of her life, and they're more apparent the more that you know her, but I don't think it's the kind of thing where you need to know what it is in order to like it. And I think there is something about it where it's for grown women. Young people also like it, but her clothes are not clothes for young teen girls.

LN: Was that also something you learned from your research?

LGF: I mean, technically, I think Miu Miu was supposed to be the younger brand, and the vibe did feel a little bit younger, but Prada always felt a little bit adult. Even when you look at the suits or the proper little silk dresses, you're like, "These are clothes for adults."

CF: I feel like that's also where the styling sneaks in. You’ll see the childlike Mary Jane with the suit and maybe one little brooch or something that is so subtle…but it's not hitting you over the head.

LGF: Yeah, because I think there's also a thing where you always look up to something. You know how you read Seventeen magazine when you're 12? And I think there is something about Prada where no matter how old you are, it still feels just beyond where you currently are.

LN: Every time I buy a piece, it still feels, in a weird way, unattainable, or I question if I'm mature or smart or cool enough.

LGF: And then you look in the mirror, and it feels like it feels exactly where you should be.

CF: It is like, "Am I a woman? Am I a glamorous woman?"

LN: Yeah. Maybe it's just because we think about her so much as the likeness of that.

CF: Well, we have made her the ideal almost. She is the one to be judged against, for so many people, but then that makes it hard to articulate what she actually is.

LGF: But even her glamour—there is something attainable about it. She doesn't wear crazy makeup, and she doesn't style her hair in any kind of way. It's just her holding her little scarf, and you think, "I need a scarf to hold!" Going back to her interest in real life, it's just so her. And who doesn't want to just be like themselves? You want to be her, but you really just want to be you.

Samsung Galaxy S27 and S27 Ultra camera specs reportedly leaked

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

If you were waiting for the upcoming Samsung Galaxy S27 lineup to drop before upgrading your smartphone, we have some bad news, particularly if you were hoping for some premium camera updates.

According to a new leak from Samsung news outlet SammyGuru, Samsung doesn't seem to have any major camera upgrades in store for the upcoming line of devices.

Another recent leak suggested that the Samsung Galaxy S27 line would see a big camera redesign. However, based on the SammyGuru report, there may not be too much new when it comes to the cameras themselves — at least for the S27 and S27 Plus models.

In fact, if you have a Samsung Galaxy S26 and are considering an upgrade to the Galaxy S27 or Galaxy S27 Plus, it looks like there will be no change to the cameras at all. Both the S27 and S27 Plus will reportedly be equipped with a 50MP main camera with a Sony IMX955 or Samsung S5KGNG sensor, SammyGuru reported. The ultrawide lens will reportedly be the same 12MP Sony IMX564, as well as the same 10MP 3x telephoto lens and 12MP selfie camera. The best tech deal this week The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile Products available for purchase through affiliate links. If you buy something through links on our site, Mashable may earn an affiliate commission.

The Apple iPhone 18 Pro is free right now with T-Mobile

The Galaxy S27 Pro and Galaxy S27 Ultra, thankfully, will see some upgrades, according to the new report. However, at this point, it's unclear just how much of an upgrade they'll be.

SammyGuru reported that the S27 Ultra and S27 Pro will both get a new 200MP main camera with a Samsung S5KHP6 sensor. The S27 Ultra's predecessor, the Samsung Galaxy S26 Ultra, has a Samsung S5KHP2 sensor, so there is a change here between the two device generations.

The S27 Pro and S27 Ultra could also receive a selfie camera upgrade with a 16MP front-facing lens, which is an upgrade from the 12MP resolution of their predecessors. Finally, SammyGuru reported that the S27 Pro will have a 3x optical zoom telephoto camera, and the S27 Ultra will have a 5x optical zoom.

Of course, the report is unconfirmed for now. Typically, Samsung phones leak heavily in the months before they launch. And with the Samsung Galaxy S27 line of smartphones expected to arrive in January or February 2027, we expect a flood of leaks in the near future.

Kemper (KMPR) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Rate actions lifted margins as management traded policy volume for profitability.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Kemper Corporation (KMPR +3.74%) reported improved sequential underwriting results as rate increases in California and other key markets began earning into the portfolio. Management stated the company is prioritizing profitability and margin restoration over policy growth, leading to an expected decline in policies in force. The company consolidated its distribution and marketing capabilities under a unified leadership team to coordinate lead generation and digital sales across product lines. Management reported that geographic diversification is a priority, with Florida and Texas representing a growing share of the personal auto book to reduce concentration in California.

Operator: Good morning, ladies and gentlemen, and welcome to Kemper's Third Quarter 2026 Earnings Conference Call. My name is Jen, and I will be your coordinator today. [Operator Instructions] As a reminder, this conference call is being recorded for replay purposes. I would now like to introduce your host for today's conference call, Michael Marinaccio, Kemper's Vice President of Corporate Development and Investor Relations. Mr. Marinaccio, you may begin.

Michael Marinaccio: Thank you. Good morning, everyone, and welcome to Kemper's discussion of our third quarter 2026 results. This morning, you'll hear from Steve McAnena, Kemper's President and CEO; and Brad Camden, Kemper's Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer. We'll make a few opening remarks to provide context around our third quarter results, followed by a Q&A session. During the interactive portion of the call, our presenters will be joined by John Boschelli, Kemper's Executive Vice President and Chief Investment Officer; Chris Flint, Kemper's Executive Vice President and Chief Distribution and Marketing Officer; Eric Kappler, Kemper's Executive Vice President and Head of P&C; and Jennifer Kopps-Wagner, Kemper's Executive Vice President and Head of Life.

After the markets closed yesterday, we issued our earnings release, filed our Form 10-Q with the SEC and published our earnings presentation and financial supplement. You can find these documents in the Investors section of our website, kemper.com. Our discussion today may contain forward-looking statements within the meaning of the safe harbor provisions of the Private Securities Litigation Reform Act of 1995. These statements include, but are not limited to, the company's outlook on its future results of operation and financial condition. Our actual future results and financial condition may differ materially from these statements. For information on additional risks that may impact these forward-looking statements, please refer to our 2025 Form 10-K and our third quarter earnings release.

This morning's discussion also includes non-GAAP financial measures we believe are meaningful to investors. In our financial supplement, earnings presentation and earnings release, we have defined and reconciled all the non-GAAP financial measures to GAAP where required in accordance with SEC rules. You can find each of these documents in the Investors section of our website, kemper.com. All comparative references will be to the corresponding 2025 period unless otherwise stated. I'll now turn the call over to Steve.

Stephen McAnena: Thanks, Michael. Good morning, everyone, and thank you for joining us. As I noted in our second quarter earnings call, we're taking actions to strengthen execution and accountability. Today, I can report that those actions are taking hold. The progress we made in the second quarter continued and our operating results improved. Two disciplines are behind these results: relentlessly reviewing our data and acting with urgency. Over the past 5 months, I spent much of my time with our employees, agents and business partners. They told me what makes Kemper special: talented people, deep relationships and a drive to serve customers the broader market often overlooks. They were just as clear about what needs to change, and we listened.

Their input, along with my own assessment, informs 3 immediate priorities: restoring profitability, positioning Kemper for durable growth and strengthening our leadership team. Together, they give us a clear path forward, a path I believe in. We'll take them one at a time, beginning with profitability because it's foundational to everything we do. Let's start with personal auto. Our rate and non-rate actions have improved the underlying combined ratio by nearly 8 points since the first quarter. That's real progress. And while we're pleased with these gains, we're not yet earning an underwriting profit, and we won't be satisfied until we do. California, our largest market, is a big part of that story.

Those same steps reduced California's underlying combined ratio by more than 7 points since the first quarter. But all of this came with a trade-off, and I want to be direct about it. We acted decisively, and we expected premium and policy counts to decline and they did. It wasn't an easy call, but it was the right one. And as margins improve, our focus turns to stabilizing production and returning to growth at a pace our profitability justifies. Turning to commercial auto. Last quarter, we said we'd put margins ahead of volume, take more rate and tighten our underwriting. Since then, margins have narrowed, underscoring the need for those actions and new business growth has slowed as we expected.

We'll stay the course and accept modest growth as we work back toward our targets. Beyond auto, Kemper Life continues to deliver dependable earnings, steady cash flow and valuable diversification. Together with our high-quality investment portfolio, it gives us a solid foundation while we improve P&C results. Restoring profitability is where we start, not where we stop. With every point of margin, we earn more of the right to grow. And that brings me to our second priority, positioning Kemper for durable growth, a kind that's profitable and built to last. We're pursuing that growth through our newly formed distribution marketing organization led by Chris Flint.

We created this structure in September with a simple objective, reach more customers more productively across multiple channels at a lower cost. Many consumers value personal guidance and service, and we intend to deepen those relationships, not replace them. At the same time, people want flexibility in how they engage. Our job is to make connecting with us seamless wherever and whenever they choose. Doing that effectively requires one team that coordinates tactics across every channel. Over the next several months, we'll run structured tests across lead generation, lead management and lead fulfillment to learn what drives results. We'll judge every tactic on performance, we'll scale what works and we'll stop what doesn't.

In time, this will become the groundwork for future growth. Strategy sets direction, but people and culture deliver the results, which brings me to our third priority, leadership. We need leaders who are highly accountable, make timely decisions and work as one company, not in silos. That's the leadership team and culture we're building at Kemper. We've added experience and depth to the team across P&C, life, claims and distribution. We're also simplifying how decisions get made and how work gets done. You won't find those changes on an income statement, but you'll see them in how quickly we move and how consistently we execute. Before I close, I want to recognize our commitment to the communities we serve.

Through the Kemper Foundation, our philanthropic arm, we sponsor Read Conmigo, a program that puts resources directly into the hands of teachers and schools to enable dual language education. It helps children build the skills and confidence they need to succeed. We bring that same spirit to our customers every day, helping people protect what matters and supporting them through some of life's toughest moments. So let me close where I began. Our actions are taking hold. We're restoring profitability with discipline, building the capabilities for durable growth and leading with the team that owns the outcome. We're encouraged by our progress, but we're not declaring victory.

There's much work ahead, and I have every confidence in this organization as we move Kemper forward. Thank you. With that, I'll turn the call over to Brad for a closer look at our financial results.

Bradley Camden: Thank you, and good morning, everyone. As Steve shared, we had a strong quarter, but we have more work to do. Net income was $52.4 million or $0.88 per diluted share and adjusted consolidated net operating income was $59.9 million or $1.01 per diluted share. Our financial results reflect continued progress in underwriting performance and consistent contributions from Life and investments. I'll begin my comments with our operating businesses, followed by expense efficiency, capital and investments. Specialty P&C reported a combined ratio of 99.2%, down 4.8 points sequentially. Reported results benefited from $23 million of favorable prior year reserve development. The underlying combined ratio also improved to 101.7% from 102.3%.

The improvement in Specialty P&C was driven largely by personal auto, where we're beginning to see the cumulative benefits of our rate and non-rate actions. The reported combined ratio improved to 99.3% compared with 105% last quarter, while the underlying combined ratio improved another 2.2 points to 103.3%. The biggest improvement in the book has been in our largest state, California. Its underlying combined ratio improved 5.2 points sequentially to 102.1%. Since the beginning of 2025, we've received approval for cumulative rate increases of approximately 13% in California personal auto, including approximately 50% in the bodily injury coverage. Bodily injury has been our greatest source of margin pressure following last year's increase in the financial responsibility limits.

These rate increases, together with our rate and non-rate actions, are contributing to improved results. We expect additional benefits as the more recent increases earn into the book. As Steve mentioned, our actions to restore profitability came with an expected trade-off, lower production. Personal auto earned premiums were $600 million, down 7% sequentially, while policies in force declined approximately 10% during the quarter. While restoring profitability remains our top priority, geographic diversification is also important to the long-term performance of personal auto. We believe a more balanced portfolio that reduces concentration risk will support more consistent underwriting results and profitable growth over time.

As we expand in other markets, we expect California to represent a smaller percentage of our personal auto portfolio over time. We're making progress toward that objective. California now represents 63% of policies compared with 67% earlier this year. Florida and Texas, our second and third largest markets, now account for 28% of personal auto policies, an increase of 5.5 percentage points since the first quarter. Together, they represent approximately 79% of new business direct written premium. Moving to commercial auto. Our decision to moderate production is reflected in slower policy growth this quarter. Policies in force increased 0.7%, while earned premiums increased 3.6% sequentially to $258 million.

The business continues to generate an underwriting profit on an underlying basis, although margins have narrowed. The reported combined ratio was 99% compared with 101.2% last quarter. The underlying combined ratio increased 4.1 points sequentially to 97.8%, reflecting elevated loss costs, primarily in bodily injury. We've taken additional pricing and underwriting actions to address these trends. We'll continue to moderate PIF growth until we're confident that earned pricing is outpacing loss trends and that margins are on a sustainable path toward our long-term targets. Turning to Life. We continue to see consistent earnings from this business. Adjusted net operating income was $20 million this quarter compared with $18 million last quarter and $19 million a year ago.

Normalized revenues increased modestly to $153 million compared with $152 million last quarter and $148 million a year ago. Average annual premium issued per policy increased more than 6% year-over-year, reflecting our disciplined approach to pricing and underwriting. Beyond the performance of our operating businesses, expense management remains an important priority. Since launching our restructuring effort in 2025, we have implemented actions generating approximately $90 million in annualized run rate savings. These efforts have helped us maintain a healthy expense ratio despite declining premium volumes. We continue to identify opportunities to simplify operations and improve efficiency. As production stabilizes and returns to growth, we expect to benefit from greater scale and continued expense discipline, supporting stronger operating leverage.

Moving to our balance sheet. Our capital and liquidity position remains strong. Parent company liquidity was $727 million at quarter end, including $91 million of cash and investments and $636 million of available borrowing capacity. Debt to capital was 28%, essentially unchanged from last quarter. Our insurance subsidiaries remain well capitalized with estimated risk-based capital ratios of 625% for life and 230% for P&C. Together, holding company liquidity and subsidiary capital provide financial flexibility to support our businesses as we execute our profitability initiatives. Turning to our investment portfolio. It remains an important source of earnings, supported by its size, diversification and credit quality.

The portfolio totaled approximately $8.3 billion, including $6.3 billion in fixed maturities, of which 69% were rated A or higher. Net investment income was $107 million compared with $105 million in the second quarter. Pretax equivalent annualized book yield on our core portfolio was 4.8% compared with average investment-grade new money yields of approximately 5.9%. With new money yields above our existing portfolio yield, we see an opportunity to boost investment income as cash flows are reinvested depending on portfolio turnover and market conditions. Overall, we're encouraged by the financial progress this quarter. We're focused on further improving underwriting profitability, building capabilities for durable growth and maintaining financial strength. With that, operator, we'd be happy to take questions.

And as we start to see that and feel comfortable, we put together a couple of good quarters, we will then start marching towards PIF stabilization, which oftentimes lags profit restoration by a couple of quarters. And so you can kind of think about 2027, making some progress to sort of drop below 100 and then a couple of quarters after that, PIF stabilization. Stephen McAnena: Great question. And I'll -- this is Steve again. I'll start off and hand it over to Chris. I'd say contextually important to understand where we're coming from before getting into an agenda. We're primarily an agent-based organization, and that's great. The challenge with that is consumers, as you know, want to interact with you whenever, wherever they want. Bradley Camden: Thanks, Paul. Great question. Maybe I'll touch briefly on our capital allocation philosophy and how we move capital within our ecosystem. We try to get capital up to the most fungible entity, which is the holding company. And at the holding company, then we can distribute the capital down to our various legal entities, whether that's on the P&C side or the life side. And so we focus on capital fungibility first. When you look specifically at the P&C subsidiary and our rated entity is Trinity, that represents -- or that has roughly a 230% RBC level. That was within our -- well within our tolerances here in the organization. We're well above regulatory minimums.

We have plenty of capital to operate in that and that legal entity and then that business. If we need more, we can always drop more down there or do what we need. But right now, with what we're seeing from improvement in profitability, a decline of written premiums, my expectation is that RBC ratio will continue to improve as we make profits and the trailing 12-month written premium comes down. But like I said, well within our normal tolerances. With respect to the debt-to-capital ratio, which is roughly 28%, it is high, higher than we'd like it to be, but it was driven by last quarter's goodwill impairment.

And so as we return to profitability and continue to make money, as we address any debt issuances, we have a couple of years out there before we get to maturity. The show's new direction continues to draw feedback.

60 Minutes has entered a new era amid the 2026 TV schedule, and a person who’s been overseeing much of the work behind the scenes is CBS News Editor-in-Chief Bari Weiss. In conjunction with the news magazine show’s EP, Nick Bilton, Weiss has been looking to update the program, and those efforts have been accompanied by major staff changes and backlash. Some of the criticisms have come from former staff members, including ex-EP Bill Owens, who recently shared his biggest concerns over the series he once shepherded.

Owens worked at CBS News for 37 years and, in 2019, he was named the EP of 60 Minutes after having spent 12 years overseeing its content in other roles. He ultimately resigned from his post in April 2025 amid a flurry of behind-the-scenes changes at 60. Since his departure, Owens has discussed his time with the show, and he notably shared thoughts ahead of the Season 59 premiere, which aired in September. At Semafor’s New Media & Marketing Playbook event, Owens was asked for his thoughts on his former workplace and said:

Not a lot. [Skydance owns] the company, they can do whatever they want. But the ratings are telling. There’s no doubt that linear television is in decline. But the numbers the other day were less than 6 million people. It’s like the evening news. 60 Minutes? That would have freaked me out.

Ratings for the latest season of 60 have reportedly been down compared to those of Season 58. The season premiere notched lower viewership and, despite some forward progress in subsequent weeks, more recent numbers haven’t been so strong. For instance, per data on the show’s October 25 broadcast, the episode only drew 5.6 million live viewers, which fell well below Season 58’s 9.1 million average. Despite that, when Bari Weiss addressed concerns, she stated that the show was doing “tremendously well.”

As for Owens, he’s been candid about leaving 60 Minutes, and he has attributed that move to no longer having the editorial independence he and his colleagues had long been granted. During the Semafor event, Owens also criticized the show’s interview with Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth and argued that better questions should’ve been asked. On a separate note, the veteran journalist also admitted that he wished his departure would’ve had a different effect on 60:

When I left, I really hoped that I was going to cause enough of a commotion that they would kind of back off. And the people, my senior team, the correspondents, they’ve tried their best. But it’s just a really difficult circumstance.

May 2026 marked the beginning of major personnel changes at 60 Minutes, as several high-profile staffers were let go, including correspondents Sharyn Alfonsi and Cecilia Vega as well as former EP Tanya Simon. Days later, fellow host Scott Pelley was fired as well after he reportedly accused Weiss of “murdering” the show. Around that time, Owens defended Pelley’s comments, saying that he “stood up the way that I did a year ago and I couldn’t be prouder of him, and I know all the people at 60 Minutes couldn’t be prouder of him.”

Bill Owens has since moved on to other ventures and, beginning in March 2027, he’ll executive-produce a YouTube-based outlet known as NewPress. It’s also been rumored that Owens will spill tea about his time at 60 Minutes through a tell-all memoir, but that’s yet to materialize. At the very least, though, Owens still seems somewhat interested about what the future holds for 60.
