Scott McTominay given police escort as drama 36 hours before Scotland World Cup clash laid bare

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Scott McTominay has revealed he had to have a police escort to a hospital in Boston to have his heart ‘shocked’ 36 hours before Scotland’s opening World Cup game against Haiti.

At the time, his absence from training and travel arrangements to Boston – he travelled from the team’s base in Charlotte separately from the rest of the team – were attributed to a stomach bug. In fact, it was a heart condition known as atrial fibrillation.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

“You're not going to say I have something to do with my heart two days before the first game of the World Cup,” he said.

“It's not an ideal scenario being in the hospital two days before—well, technically 36 hours before the first game of the World Cup. But you have to take the cards that you're dealt on the chin. It's part of the game. There's nothing I could do about it.”

Some will question the decision to let him play in not only the match against Haiti, which Scotland won 1-0, but also the two following group games against Morocco and Brazil. The Scots lost both fixtures and exited the World Cup with McTominay perceived to have been under par.

“It was in my hands,” he said. “The medical team reassured me it was safe to play and they said: ‘What do you think?' I said: ‘Let’s go for it’.”

Unbeknown to nearly everyone, and while Scotland fans were drinking bars dry in Boston, McTominay was at the centre of incredible drama.

Manager Steve Clarke told reporters that the midfielder had been laid low by a stomach bug. The reality of the situation - and the fact it was kept secret for so long - is barely believable given the global attention on the tournament and that McTominay is Scotland's star player.

He himself sought to downplay the situation on Tuesday at Hampden as he revealed the true story for the first time. “We got a police escort to go and get the shock then that was it to be honest,” recalled McTominay. “After that, I was back in normal rhythm, which is how a healthy heart should be then I went and played the game two days later.”

He described being put under anaesthetic. “You have to get put to sleep,” he said. “Whenever you go under anaesthetic and you have to get put to sleep, it decreases the athletic performance a little bit and (affects) sleep and stuff like that. It was far from ideal but those are the cards that you're dealt, there's nothing you can do about it.”

Although he stressed his family were fully on board with him playing, he wouldn’t have listened had it been otherwise. “All my family and friends had just flown 10 hours for the first game of the World Cup,” he said.

“I didn’t sit there thinking I might not play. That would never be the case. I was always going to play in that game. Maybe selfishly, I don’t know but that’s football. Players play with injuries all the time when they’re not quite right and it might be to the detriment of the team. (But) I was fully functional.”

The Napoli midfielder has since undergone an operation after the problem resurfaced following an early season Serie A game against Como. He returned to Manchester, where he had a procedure known as pulsed field ablation.

The player was sidelined for several weeks, missing the first games under new Scotland manager Sebastien Pocognoli but has returned to the squad for upcoming Nations League games against North Macedonia and Switzerland.

Leah McKendrick To Follow ‘Voicemails For Isabelle’ With “Ferociously Female” Thriller ‘Delulu’ Starring Maddie Ziegler; XYZ & Temple Hill Produce — AFM

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Here’s a buzzy one. Leah McKendrick, fresh off her Netflix smash Voicemails For Isabelle, is next set to direct thriller Delulu, in which she’ll also star alongside Pretty Lethal and West Side Story star Maddie Ziegler.

McKendrick has also written and will serve as an executive producer on the project which heralds from XYZ Films (Havoc) and Off Campus and Twilight producer Temple Hill.

Described as a “bloody showdown about fame, envy and the strange intimacy of girlhood”, McKendrick “will play a washed-up millennial pop singer who kidnaps a rising Gen Z influencer (Ziegler) and makes one deranged demand: get her a million followers in two weeks or die.” Related Stories News Daisy Ridley, Darren Criss, Travis Fimmel & Judy Greer Join Catherine Zeta-Jones In Tate Taylor's Marriage Counselor Thriller 'Cupid'; Filming Underway -- AFM News 'Maxton Hall' Star Damian Hardung & 'GOT' Alum Charlotte Hope Set For Thriller 'Human Remains' -- AFM

Wyck Godfrey, Marty Bowen, Isaac Klausner and Laura Quicksilver are producing for Temple Hill. Hal Sadoff is exec-producing. Guy Danella is producing for XYZ, which is financing and launching sales at this week’s AFM. Watch on Deadline

Voicemails For Isabelle, released on Netflix this summer, became the best-performing rom-com on the streamer in the last five years. The Zoey Deutch, Nick Robinson and McKendrick starrer spent seven weeks in Netflix’s top five global movies and scored an 88% score on Rotten Tomatoes.

McKendrick also wrote, directed and starred in Scrambled, which premiered at SXSW and was released theatrically by Lionsgate and Roadside Attractions.

Set for production in 2027, McKendrick said of Delulu and former Dance Moms and music video star Ziegler: “I have been a die-hard fan of Maddie since she was a little girl dancing her heart out on our TV screens – and I’m beyond blown away by the multifaceted artist she has grown into. I’m fired up to watch her devour a role that demands every color of her insane talent. Temple Hill and XYZ are fearlessly championing my vision to make my rawest, bloodiest, most ferociously female film yet.”

“Leah is a bold storyteller with a voice and vision that transcends genre,” said Laura Quicksilver, EVP of Development, Temple Hill Entertainment. “We are thrilled to be collaborating with her on Delulu, a film that is outrageously funny, emotionally vulnerable and just wickedly twisted. Leah has revealed herself as a real talent both behind and in front of the camera and we can’t wait for audiences to see her go head-to-head with Maddie Ziegler, who brings a magnetism and fearlessness to all that she does. This role finally weaves together Maddie’s immense talents in both acting and dance in a way we’ve all been clamoring for.”

Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Remake Water Temple Walkthrough

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Nintendo Switch 2The Legend of ZeldaThe Legend of Zelda: Ocarina Of Time

Well, you’ve made it. You’re finally at the most infamous dungeon in all of Zelda-dom, the Water Temple of Ocarina of Time. Good news for you: In the remake, this dungeon is far, far easier than it’s ever been. A few of the edges have been sanded down, the rooms all look far more distinct and memorable so it’s less of a mental maze, and the iron boots can be equipped and de-equipped simply by pressing down on the gamepad. Compared to the original, it’s a smooth ride. But still, this is one of the most involved dungeons in the game, so buckle on that Zora Tunic and let’s get swimming.

To enter the Water Temple, you need to be wearing (no, I wasn’t joking) the Zora Tunic, which you get by freeing King Zora from his ice block back in Zora Domain. To do that, you need Blue Fire in a bottle, which you obtain in the Ice Cavern as an adult. You’ll also need the Iron Boots from that same cavern. If you haven’t gotten that far yet, back yourself up and come back to this guide later.

Once you’re in the temple, remove your iron boots and float to the top. Run forward, and you’ll be in a big, open room with a bunch of doors on all four sides across four different floors, and water below you. I’m going to be referring to this as the “main hall” going forward. We’re going to be here a lot. I’ll also be referring to doors by floor and cardinal direction, so if you’re ever confused about what I’m talking about, open that map.

The first thing you’re going to do is slay that Blue Tektite in front of you. Sword, hookshot, whatever you like, just get it out of the way.

Next, you’re going to equip those Iron Boots, descend to the first floor, and head through the east door. Once you’re inside, remove the boots to float upward for a cutscene with your fiancee, Ruto. When it’s over, grab the Dungeon Map from the chest in front of you, and then stand on the platform with the Triforce symbol and play Zelda’s Lullaby to lower the water level.

Real quick tip: There are three different spots to mess with the water level in this dungeon, and each one will raise it to a different spot. This one brings it down to the first floor, and is marked by glowy blue pillars. It might be good to make a note somewhere of which color results in which water level, just in case you ever get turned around or confused and the water level is in the wrong place.

Once the water has dropped, fearlessly jump down the hole, landing in the water below so it doesn’t damage you. Now, with the water taken care of, note that one of the torches in here is lit. Stand behind it with your bow equipped and aim an arrow through the fire to each of the two torches on the other end of the room to open the door in front of you, then enter. Defeat the three Spikes by blocking their attacks with your shield and swinging your sword when they’re vulnerable to obtain a Small Key. (Din’s Fire is also usable for this puzzle).

Backtrack the way you came in and head out into the main hall. There are two more aptly named Spikes out here waiting for you; get rid of them. To your left (west side, 1F) is a bombable/breakable wall. Knock that out, and use your Iron Boots to sink, sink, sink. Head down the underwater corridor and take care of the Shell Blade by hitting it with your hookshot when its mouth is all the way open. At the end of the corridor, take off the boots and rise up.

In here, climb the platform jutting out of the water and use the hookshot to grapple onto the pillars ahead of you. Take down the Tektites and the Like Like (Hookshot is great here), and you’ll be rewarded with the Compass. Now, all the way back to the main hall!

To the west (1F) is a block you can push forward until it drops in the water. Do that, then use the boots to sink again. Look out for the Shell Blade in the way, then rise back up at the end of the hall. You’ll find yourself in a room with a whirlpool and some statues underwater, and a chest on the other side.

This looks trickier than it is. Use the iron boots to drop into the whirlpool, aiming for the center platform (the serpent’s body) stretched down the middle of the pool. Stand on the platform and use your hookshot to smack the weird gem thing in the serpent’s mouth. This will unlock the underwater grate, allowing you to enter. You might need to don and doff the iron boots a few times to swim over properly, but with some careful aiming it’s very doable. There are two more Shell Blades to fight inside, after which you’re free to take off the boots, rise to the surface, and grab the small key.

Back across the whirlpool, out the door, into the main hall again. There’s a locked door on the central pillar here, directly in front of you, and you now have a key. Head inside.

Once inside, step forward, turn around, and look up for a grapple point. Use your hookshot to get on the platform above. Right in front of you is a spot to stand and play Zelda’s Lullaby to raise the water back up. (Note: the columns here are green, green brings the water to 2F! Write it down if you need to!) Before you leave this inside pillar, though, jump in the water and look down: a new gap has opened up with the water level rising.

Use iron boots to drop down, where three Spikes are waiting for you. Kill them with the hookshot, then use the hookshot to hit the green switch above your head, releasing a Shell Blade. Kill that too, then remove the iron boots where it was standing to rise up into the now-open area. There’s a red rupee in a chest on the way up, as well as a gold skulltula on the wall opposite. Funny, I feel like something else used to be here in the original game…anyway, grab these, backtrack the way you came in, and take off the boots to rise back up into the middle of the pillar. Then swim out the door into the main hall.

Directly in front of you across the water (2F, east side) is a bombable/hammerable wall in an alcove. You know what to do. Smash the pots for bombs if needed, otherwise use the Megaton Hammer if you’ve cleared the Fire Temple already, and head on through.

Be cautious as you head into the water and equip the Iron Boots. There are four Stingers waiting for you. Your hookshot will take care of them, but it’s easy to get overwhelmed, so take it slow (not that you can do otherwise with those boots on). Once they’re dead, proceed to the end of the underwater hallway and unequip the boots to rise to the surface.

In front of you is a pool of water with a switch in it, and a round platform being lifted by a fountain. Use your hookshot to hit the switch to lower the platform, stand on the platform, then use hookshot again on the same switch to raise yourself up to the floor above. Your reward is a Small Key. Take it, then backtrack all the way to the main hall again.

In the main hall, hop in the water and swim around the central pillar to the locked door directly across from where you just entered. It’s got glowing red columns around itâcan’t miss it. Use the Small Key and head inside. Smack the switch and ready yourself; a blue Tektite will descend and try to fight you. Once it’s dispatched, stand on the lowered platform and use your hookshot to hit the switch again and rise up.

It’s another Zelda’s Lullaby spot, this time with red lights, that will bring the water to 3F! Play that same song! Up goes the water. Jump down from the ledge next to the Triforce symbol and back into the water of the main room.

Mind any Tektites you haven’t dispatched already and swim on over to 3F, west side, for another locked door for which you conveniently have a key.

A couple of Keese will be waiting for you to slash them up. Take stock of your surroundings before you proceed. You’ll want to use the hookshot to grapple your way up these moving platforms to the top, one by one. Start by dropping down to the platform below the one you start on, and it’ll descend until you can grapple across. Then just work your way up, one by one. Don’t panic, it’s very doable, and there’s a stable platform to the side you can jump to if you need a second to collect yourself. Head through the door at the top once you reach it.

In this next room, I started by hitting all the Tektites below with my arrows just to get them out of the way. Then, use your hookshot to hit the switch at the center of the room and make the grapple points raise up. You’ll want to grapple to the first point directly in front of you, then (counterintuitively) hit the switch again to lower the points. Use the grapple point on the next wall to crossâyou’ll be able to continue along the ledge only if the grapple point pillars are lowered. Hit the switch yet again and grapple to the final pillar, hit the switchÂ again to lower the pillars. Climb atop the one next to you, then hit the switch one final time to raise the pillar and lift you within reach of the next floor.

Hit those pots for health, arrows, whatever before you go through the door, it’s time for one of the best bosses in the game!

Shadow Link is tough; he mirrors your moves! Don’t attack him head-on while his shield is up or he’ll do something I hear the kids call “aura farming.” I was able to beat him pretty easily by keeping my guard up and dodging his combos, waiting for an opening. In the first phase of the fight, I would dodge to the left side twice whenever he swung, then would come in for a combo toward his exposed back. In the second phase, this was trickier, but still very doable if I dodged backwards twice and then threw out the hookshot to stun him. Make sure you dodge backwards far enough when he gears up for a spin attack, and don’t let yourself get pushed too close to the arena walls, or he can pin you down for some big damage.

With Shadow Link down, head through the door opposite where you came in (use your map if you got turned around) to finally obtain the Longshot, which will let you hookshot targets even farther away than before! To escape this room, aim for the very far away targets above you, then drop down the waterfall into the cavern below. Let yourself float down the river, but mind the whirlpoolsâyou can use the iron boots to just walk between them underwater. About three-quarters of the way down the river, there’s a gold skulltula on a wall above you. Make sure you’re firmly iron-booted underwater, then use the longshot to smack it once to defeat it, and then again to collect the token.

Climb atop the stone outcropping on the end of the river and use the longshot again, once to hit the eye above the gates and open them, and again to grapple to the grapple point on the other side of the gates before they close on you.

We’re back in familiar territory, so head out to the main hall once again. We’ve still got some work to do, and we’re sorely in want of a boss key right about now. First, we need to get the water level down. Get those boots on and drop down to 1F east, the room where you first ran into Ruto. Swim up to the Triforce and play Zelda’s Lullaby to drop the water, then head back into the main hall. Then, you want to enter the central pillar again from the door on 1F. We’re here to raise the water level, but hold up. Grapple to the Triforce stand, then look at the ceiling for a grapple point you can now reach with the longshot. Fly on up there, then look across to the other side of the ceiling for another gold skulltula. Once you have that, drop back down and play Zelda’s Lullaby.

Everything’s in place! Exit the pillar and run around the central platform to stand on the floating block, looking across at the outer wall. You should see a gate with a grapple point on the other side, and an eye switch above. Once again, use the longshot to hit the eye, then immediately use it again on the grapple to get through. Drop into the pool and equip the iron boots, then walk into the hallway. There’s a movable block wedged into the wall hereâpull it out as far as you can, blocking off the hallway, then take off the boots and swim up and out of the pool. You should be able to see a fence ahead of you with a waterfall on the other side, and a grapple point on the ceiling. Grapple to it with the longshot, use the iron boots to sink into the water, then head down the hallway and push the block out of your way to clear a path to the chest with yet another small key.

Back to the main hall again, equipping the iron boots again and sinking down to 1F, north. Grapple across the gap and use your small key on the door. Inside, there are a lot of tektites in the way. Use your sword, shield, bow, whatever, to get them out of the way (seriously, they will knock you into whirlpools otherwise). Carefully cross the room, avoiding the boulders, using the iron boots as needed to stay out of whirlpools, and go through the door. In here, use the longshot to dispatch all the stingers, then smash the rock walls (with bombs or the megaton hammer) to reveal a block that can be pushed into the water to hit a switch. The switch will raise the water level for you to head out the door in the wall above.

Whew. Almost done. In here, step on the switch to raise platforms you can jump across, and a tektite with them. Get rid of it (and the other tektite on the other side of the room) as you see fit, then cross and go through the opening. On the other side, be careful. Boulders are rolling down this stream and will run you over if you step in at the wrong time. Wait for one to pass, then go under the waterfall and immediately turn to the left to jump into the alcove. There’s a shell blade waiting, so get that out of the way. Before you head through the door, turn back, wait for the boulder to pass, then jump back in the stream, through the waterfall, and look upâthere’s another gold skulltula near the ceiling. Grab it fast, then turn back and head through the door to, at long last, claim the boss key.

Great work, folks! Turn back, follow the boulders down the stream and into the room you were in earlier with the boulders and whirlpools, then back into the main hall. I’m sorry to say we’ve got to mess with the water levels one more time. You’ll need to go back into 2F west, head up the fountain, and play Zelda’s Lullaby one last time to raise the water and open a path up the big stairs to the north on 2F (technically 3F). Drop down, and head that direction. You’ll have to stand on the platform of the central pillar and longshot your way over. Load up on hearts from the pots before you go in, and get ready to fight.

Okay, not yet. Use your iron boots in this room to climb the waterfall and avoid the spiky things spinning back and forth.Â Now get ready to fight!

Morpha isn’t too bad, as bosses go. The boss itself is the weird red Metroid-looking ball thingie that’s hiding in the tentacles. Your main goal is to use the longshot to rip it out of its watery protection and whack it a bunch with your sword. This is pretty easy with Navi’s targeting; the main thing you need to worry about is getting hit by water tentacles. Morpha will manifest these out of the water and try and slam them on the ground near you. Early on, they’ll be pretty spread out, but later in the fight they’ll form a big wall that’s harder to avoid. If you get trapped you can also use the grapple points on the sides of the room to fly out of danger. I tried to stay on the edges as much as possible; you never really want to be in the water with Morpha. But you can use the central platforms to bait Morpha into an easier spot for you to longshot the ball out, or just to cross out of the way of attacks. Try to be aware of where Morpha the Ball is at all times, as this fight can get annoying if it’s always all the way across the room, just out of range. Use those grapple points!

That’s the Water Temple done, folks! Enjoy the cutscenes, and get the heck outta there to see how the Zora are doing.

Oh, psst. Once the cutscenes are over, you’ll be at Lake Hylia, just before sunrise. Check out the stone monument in front of you to make good use of that timing. You’re welcome!

DC Studios Exit: James Gunn and Peter Safran Spent Big, Lost Money and Didn’t Hook Audiences

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

After “Superman” landed in theaters last summer, former Warner Bros. Discovery CEO David Zaslav was ready to pop Champagne. Although the movie had only been playing on the big screen for three days, he touted the “bold 10-year plan” of DC Studios co-chiefs James Gunn and Peter Safran and promised “the DC vision is clear” and “the momentum is real.”

Fast forward a year and a half, and those plans have imploded. Gunn and Safran were dismissed from the top of the comic book empire, with Marvel Studios alum Jeremy Latcham taking over as president and chief creative officer of DC Studios. Related Stories Korea Box Office: Anime Phenomenon 'Chiikawa' Staves Off Corporate Thriller 'Final Interview' to Retain Top Spot Korea Box Office: Anime Hit 'Chiikawa' Retains Lead as Brad Pitt Thriller 'Heart of the Beast' Debuts

The duo’s ouster wasn’t a total shock, even though they had been assured they were part of David Ellison’s business leadership team at Skydance after Paramount’s $111 billion acquisition of WBD closed in October. Had the two stayed, the org change would have represented a demotion. Under the previous regime, they answered directly to Zaslav and not movie co-chairs Michael De Luca and Pamela Abdy. However, Ellison and co-CEO Ynon Kreiz made plans to shift Gunn and Safran’s reporting structure into new Skydance Motion Picture Group chiefs Dana Goldberg and Josh Greenstein.

The executive shuffle is yet another setback for DC Studios, which has struggled for years to compete with Disney’s Marvel Cinematic Universe. Their short-lived leadership stint at DC follows multiple turnovers over the past decade, starting with comic book veterans Geoff Johns and Jon Berg and then Walter Hamada. After DC’s last iteration of superhero movies flamed out with duds like “The Flash” and “Aquaman and the Lost Kingdom,” Gunn and Safran were hired in 2022 to overhaul some of the company’s most lucrative IP. Efforts to right the ship were mixed at best — with tentpoles and television shows that didn’t always generate enough excitement to justify their price tags. Sources suggest the thinking was they spent big — and lost too much — on projects that just weren’t resonating with audiences.

Gunn’s “Superman,” starring David Corenswet — the duo’s first big swing as bosses — was touted as a hit with $618 million against a $225 million budget. Those ticket sales were promising enough, given the general decline in popularity of comic book movies. But they didn’t necessarily signal the kind of lasting enthusiasm that spawns an interconnected universe. Momentum all but evaporated with July’s money-loser “Supergirl,” which flopped with $126 million globally against a $170 million budget. And while October’s “Clayface” performed modestly with $109 million against a $44 million budget, sources suggest the body-horror thriller won’t break even until the studio factors in downstream revenues.

Insiders have raised eyebrows at Gunn and Safran’s decision to focus on lesser-known characters, like Superman’s cousin Kara El-Zor, aka Supergirl, and the Batman villain Clayface, rather than building out the franchise with marquee names. Although superhero movies have been hit or miss, the ones that spotlight notable characters can still pack a box-office punch. July’s “Spider-Man: Brand New Day” became the third-biggest movie in history with $2.5 billion, and December’s “Avengers: Endgame,” which brings back just about all of Earth’s Mightiest Heroes, could challenge the web-slinging Marvel sequel’s box office records.

“The industry has taken away the wrong lessons from Marvel’s success,” a studio insider tells Variety. “Higher budgets don’t equal quality storytelling.”

Television efforts, too, were uneven. DC’s main small-screen success was “The Penguin,” a “Batman” spinoff led by Colin Farrell, was greenlit before Gunn and Safran took over. But the rest of the slate has generated less fanfare. Gunn’s “Peacemaker” series, anchored by John Cena, debuted in 2022 before he stepped into the studio job. Although initial season was popular, the second season – which launched three years later – failed to match the buzz of the first. “Lanterns,” their first live-action TV show, had posted decent viewership. Yet an insider at the company suggests the crime drama isn’t quite capturing the zeitgeist, pointing out the show didn’t get renewed for a second season.

“People cannot wait to make another season of a hit show,” the source said.

Safran, a steward of New Line’s wildly profitable “Conjuring” universe and producer on “Shazam!” and “Aquaman,” was used to cutting checks and poring over balance sheets before he was hired at DC. However Gunn, best known for directing Marvel’s “Guardians of the Galaxy” trilogy, was always a curious choice for the job. Gunn had a tricky balancing act, needing to shepherd his own directorial efforts while co-managing a slate. Collaborators praised Gunn’s ability to work quickly and efficiently on simultaneous projects. But even he admitted the challenge of needing to multitask and handle the “corporate” nature of the gig.

“The thing my ADHD hyperfocus has had the most difficulty with has been dividing my time between projects I’m incredibly passionate about and the ones I’m not so much and all the things I have to do as a studio head,” Gunn, who regularly converses with comic book enthusiasts on social media, wrote on X after his ouster.

The pair will remain involved with DC, though with less authority. Gunn is directing next summer’s “Superman: Man of Tomorrow,” and the two are producing the 2027 television series “The People v. Gorilla Grodd,” with Skyler Gisondo as his “Superman” character Jimmy Olsen and Jimmy Tatro as Gorilla Grodd, and another season of “Creature Commandos.”

It’s unclear what will become of Gunn and Safran’s decade-long creative strategy of sequels, spinoffs and stand-alone stories. After “Superman: Man of Tomorrow,” which returns Corenswet’s Man of Steel and Nicholas Hoult’s supervillain Lex Luther to the forefront, the only other movie in the pipeline is “The Batman Part II.” After several delays, the sequel is scheduled to be released in 2028 — six years after Robert Pattinson’s original Caped Crusader adventure. Their oft-teased “Wonder Woman” reboot never got off the ground. And though Gunn has hinted he’s writing another DC-related project, it’ll be up to Latcham to decide whether to continue with the vision of the prior regime.

Internally, there’s hope that Latcham — an executive who helped shape the MCU — will get back to basics with DC.

“Jeremy is the kind of guy who would go to Comic-Con to get an autograph from someone,” says another source, “not someone who wants to be there signing autographs.”

A $35 Million Home Just Hit San Francisco’s Market, Marking the Most Expensive Listing of the Year

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

San Francisco’s most expensive home of the year just hit the market and is expected to draw interest from potential buyers flush with cash from the AI boom.

The home at 2770 Broadway officially went on the market on Monday with a nearly $35 million price tag.

The seven-bedroom, 6.5-bathroom home is already drawing inquiries, listing agent Neil Ward of Compass told SFGate.

Located in the exclusive Gold Coast neighborhood, the home sits on a 50-foot-wide, double-width lot, with breathtaking views of the San Francisco Bay, Golden Gate Bridge, Palace of Fine Arts, and other famed landmarks, according to the listing.

The home was designed in 1894 by Reid & Reid, the same architectural firm responsible for the iconic Fairmont Hotel. While many Gold Coast homes of this age, most of which are generational properties, need complete renovations after being sold, Ward told SFGate that the potential buyer of 2770 Broadway can “walk in with their toothbrush and their furniture.”

A 2007 renovation by architect Jim Jennings and Van Acker Construction brought together the home’s original turn-of-the-century grandeur with the functionality needed for modern family living.

The home’s 9,000 square feet are spread across four floors, where prospective buyers will find herringbone-patterned floors, arched openings, and fine millwork, all evidence of the home’s architectural greatness.

The property also features a two-car garage, wine cellar, and sport court in the rear of the house.

The home was purchased by San Francisco business executive David Chamberlain in 1989, according to SFGate. He was CEO of companies such as Shaklee, Stride Rite, and Genesco, but also had a deep connection with his city, serving as president of the San Francisco Opera and the San Francisco Chamber of Commerce.

After his death earlier this year, Chamberlain’s two daughters decided to sell the home.

Ward told SFGate that he thinks the property will sell for its $35 million asking price, which would make it the most expensive home sold on the multiple listing service in San Francisco this year.

“It is a really, really rare product that does not hit the market,” Ward said.

Between the home’s turnkey condition and the city’s competitive ultraluxury home market, Ward thinks the home’s time on the market will be brief.

“I’ve done this for over 35 years. I was here for the dot-com boom and everything in between. Nothing comes close to what’s taking place right now,” Ward said.

San Francisco is experiencing what some real estate brokerages are calling a “mansion shortage,” according to NPR. The AI boom, much of which is concentrated in San Francisco, has caused demand for luxury single-family homes in the area to skyrocket, while the supply remains stagnant.

Home to major AI companies such as OpenAI and Anthropic, the San Francisco market had a median of just 37 days on the market, according to Realtor.com data, two weeks less than the national median of 61 days.

Some listings have had over 50 offers, and 44 homes were sold for at least $1 million over asking in June alone, according to a report from NPR.

In October, the sale of a home in San Francisco’s Presidio neighborhood broke the record for the highest sale above asking. The 1966 midcentury home was listed for $18.5 million and sold for $28.5 million, an eye-watering $10 million premium.

Ross Douthat’s ’60 Minutes’ Interview Gets Tackled By Football Comparisons

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Ross Douthat was dynamite. The ratings — potentially less so.

The new “60 Minutes” correspondent garnered many huzzahs for his interlocution of President Donald Trump on the show’s Sunday telecast, but viewership fell notably from the previous week and from the year-earlier episode, both of which were boosted by NFL lead-ins.

Even so, there is some cause for cheer. In a season when the show has faced challenges luring viewers without an NFL game before it, the ratings were the highest generated without a football telecast to help kick things off. Related Stories 'Conclave’ Director Edward Berger Teases Upcoming Brad Pitt A24 Film ‘The Riders’: It’s a ‘European Odyssey’ Korea Box Office: Anime Phenomenon 'Chiikawa' Staves Off Corporate Thriller 'Final Interview' to Retain Top Spot

Douthat peppered Trump not only with questions tied to the loss Republicans suffered in the recent midterm elections, but also stopped him with inquiries about whether the president felt he would go to heaven or hell, and whether he could refrain from taunting and insulting his opponents it it means doing something for the greater good of the nation.

The literati noticed, with notables like Fox News commentator Brit Hume and CNN media analyst Brian Stelter praising Douthat’s interview.

But the show’s audience for its eighth episode of the season fell to 7.8 million viewers overall, and 918,000 between the ages of 25 and 54 — the demographic coveted most by advertisers. The overall figure marked a 26% decline from the previous week, when an NFL football game airing before the show drove 11 million viewers to the program. And the demo audience tumbled 69% from the previous year, when the show won nearly 2 million viewers between 25 and 54.

The numbers are also down from the same episode from the previous week, when the telecast lured an average of nearly 10.5 million overall and 2.23 million in the demo — representing declines of 30% and 59%, respectively.

Both the week-earlier and season-earlier episodes were lifted by NFL games that aired ahead of the first tick of the “60 Minutes” stopwatch. Sunday’s program was not.

CBS News remains under intense pressure to demonstrate stability at “60,” which was overhauled in haphazard fashion at the end of last season, despite mustering a notable 9% uptick in overall viewership. Bari Weiss, CBS News’ editor in chief, ousted Tanya Simon, an executive producer with years of history working at the show, in favor of Nick Bilton, a journalist and screenwriter with less formal TV experience. Also pushed out were correspondents Scott Pelley, Cecilia Vega and Sharyn Alfonsi. Anderson Cooper left of his own volition following the end of his contract. Their replacements, which include Douthat and contributors like Ariel Levy and Sebastian Junger, have top-shelf backgrounds, but also little experience working on a weekly TV program that emphasizes long-form enterprise journalism.

Sunday’s telecast also included an appearance by Trevor Phillips, who was recently named senior global affairs correspondent for CBS News. He interviewed far-right National Rally leader Marine Le Pen amid protests by French citizens.

Kia launches two new PV5 electric van variants in the UK

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Kia opened orders for two new PV5 electric van variants, the PV5 Chassis and PV5 Cargo L2/H2, in the UK.

The PV5 is Kia’s first fully electric van from its Platform Beyond Vehicle (PBV) business. Built on Hyundai Motor’s dedicated modular skateboard platform, E-GMP.S, Kia can easily swap body styles to enable “limitless possibilities.”

So far, the PV5 has mostly been available in Passenger and Cargo body styles with several different variations.

Kia added the PV5 Chassis, an open-bed model that can be converted into just about anything. In the UK, Kia is offering conversions through TGS Automotive Group, including lightweight tipper, Dropside, and Box solutions.

The PV5 Chassis is available exclusively with the long-range 71.2 kWh battery in the UK, which Kia said could offer a potential WLTP range of up to 258 miles (416 km).

For reference, the PV5 Cargo L2/H1, which uses the same 71.2 kWh battery, offers a WLTP range of up to 258 miles (416 km). Other markets will also have a standard-range 51.1 kWh option.

The Chassis model is 4,610 mm long, 1,895 mm wide, and 1,935 mm tall, with a 2,995 mm wheelbase.

Its compact size and 5.5-meter turning radius, Kia said, make it easy to maneuver in city streets, construction sites, and other tight work areas.

Inside, the PV5 Chassis features two seats. Like other variants, it includes a 12.9″ navigation screen and a 7.5″ driver display with standard wireless Apple CarPlay and Android Auto.

The Chassis model supports DC fast charging up to 120 kW, enabling the battery to charge from 10% to 80% in about 30 minutes. Using an 11 kW AC supply, it takes about 6 hours and 30 minutes to charge from 10% to 100%.

Payload depends on the configuration. Kia said the Dropside offers a payload of up to 633 kg, while the Tipper can carry 561 kg.

In the UK, the PV5 Chassis is now on sale from £32,916 ($43,400) on the road, including the expected £5,000 ($6,700) UK Zero Emission Van Grant.

However, it’s an “incomplete vehicle,” as Kia describes it. The converted PV5 Dropside is priced from £40,110 ($53,500) on the road, including the ZEVG, while the PV5 Tipper starts at £41,310 ($55,000).

Kia also launched the larger PV5 Cargo L2/H2 in the UK. The new variant is 4,695mm long, 1,895mm wide, and 2,200mm high, with a 2,995mm wheelbase.

The taller roof opens up an internal cargo-area height of 1,815mm. It also offers a greater load capacity than the L1/H1 and L2/H2, with 5.2 cubic meters.

It’s available in three- and four-door configurations with the choice of two or three seats. The three-door versions have a maximum payload of 630 kg, while the four-door models have dual sliding doors and offer a payload of up to 600 kg. Both versions can tow up to 1,500 kg.

Like the Chassis model, the PV5 Cargo L2/H2 is available in the UK exclusively with the 71.2 kWh battery, which provides a WLTP range of up to 239 miles.

Inside, it features the same setup as the other PV5 variants, including a 12.9″ touchscreen navigation screen and a 7.5″ driver display.

The base PV5 Cargo L2/H2 Essential two-seat starts at £35,016 ($46,700), on the road, including ZEVG.

High-Protein Packaged Foods May Not Be As Healthy As They Look

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Of the three major macronutrients—protein, carbs, and fat—protein has the best reputation, and it’s fairly well deserved. We need significant amounts of protein in our diet, and nothing particularly bad happens if we get too much. Dietary protein helps you build muscle, and if you’re trying to lose weight it helps stop you from losing too much muscle along with the fat. It also helps to make meals feel filling. Great! But.

Once the general public gets the idea that something is good for you, the companies that make packaged food start putting it in everything. Or, they make products where the good-for-you thing features prominently on the label, whether or not there is very much inside. So a lot of packaged food now boasts protein on the label. Here’s what you should ask yourself about those foods:

Take a close look here—sometimes a food will boast of a certain amount of protein per package but the package contains two or three servings. Other times, the protein per serving is correct, and we're supposed to be excited to have, say, 7 grams per serving. That's just not very much—and certainly not worth paying extra or choosing a less-delicious food just for those few added grams.

It’s good to know how much protein you need in your diet. If you’re not gaining or losing weight, and not doing very much exercise, a 150-pound person only needs about 54 grams of protein a day. (I've done the math for various body weights here.) If you’re working out a lot, or if you’re trying to gain or lose weight (yep, either way), that same 150-pound person will need more like 81 to 95 grams of protein per day. More is OK, but not necessary.

So if you’re trying to get 90 grams per day, 30 grams per meal is a reasonable amount to shoot for. Coincidentally, 30 grams is the amount of protein that tends to make a meal feel satisfying. You can do the math to figure out whether a protein-added product is going to be helpful in your day’s diet.

I think this is the most important question about protein-added foods. It’s not hard to get protein in a meal; meats, dairy, tofu, beans, and grains all tend to supply plenty. So what is this protein-added thing you’re considering, and are you eating it instead of a traditional meal?

Here’s an example. If I’m heading straight from the gym to run some errands, and I need something to substitute for a meal—yeah, this isn’t great planning, but sometimes it happens—a protein bar with 20 grams of protein is a step in the right direction. Much better than a candy bar, anyway.

But then there are things like this "protein mix" trail mix: 7 grams of protein with 200 calories. A double serving would be 400 calories and only 14 grams of protein. Hardly a great choice if your goal is just to add to your protein intake for the day.

And then there are the chips that my kid, for a time, couldn't stop eating. He loves Doritos and such; on a whim, we bought him some protein chips (yes, these are a real thing). Are they better for him than the equivalent amount of Doritos? Arguably, sort of yes. But then he started arguing that since they have protein, he should be able to eat them in place of dinner. We nixed that, but admit it: Are you having that same argument with yourself, feeling better about a snack food because it contains extra protein? If so, the marketing is working—you’re feeling good about your supposedly healthy choices, even though you’re just eating a bag of chips.

‘Tangles’ Cast Members Seth Rogen, Julia Louis-Dreyfus & Bryan Cranston On Animated Film’s Rare Back Story

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

An animated film takes time to make. An animated film based on a funny-but-deeply-moving graphic memoir about a family’s experience with early-onset Alzheimer’s disease can take a bit longer.

Lauren Miller Rogen, wife of Seth Rogen and one of the producers of the film Tangles, told the crowd at Monday night’s special screening of the film in New York that work began on the adaptation back in 2016. It was acquired earlier this year by IFC, which will release it for a qualifying run later this month ahead of a wider bow in early 2027.

“I saw so much of my own story in [author] Sarah Levitt’s family story,” Rogen said. “I have a feeling a lot of you will see your own stories in it as well.” Related Stories News AMC Global Media Posts Mixed Q3, Previews 'The Walking Dead' AMC+ Bow News Martin Scorsese Explains How Seth Rogen "Ruined" His 'Cleopatra' Film: "Kim Jong Un Doesn't Have A Sense Of Humor"

The regrettable ubiquity of dementia and Alzheimer’s, along with the potency of Levitt’s work, drew a crowd of A-listers to the project. The voice cast includes Seth Rogen, Bryan Cranston, Julia Louis-Dreyfus, Abbi Jacobson and Beanie Feldstein. Producers include Louis-Dreyfus; Rogen via his company, Point Grey; Monarch Media, Giant Ant; and LYLAS Pictures. Watch on Deadline

During a Q&A after the screening, Louis-Dreyfus said the black-and-white, 2-D film “took advantage of the art form in an unusual and whatever-the-opposite-of-derivative-is way was very appealing to me. I think it’s very funny and very devastating. I related to it from a personal point of view, having Alzheimer’s in my own family, not once but twice. So, I felt like I understood it and the story needed to be told, and I was honored to be a part of it.”

Cranston observed, “It’s not always the case when you get contacted by people you know and friends saying, ‘We’d like you to be in our movie’ that it’s good.” As the audience laughed, Seth Rogen cut in: “Almost never!” Cranson, with half-joking affirmation from Louis-Dreyfus, continued, “You go, ‘Whoa, how do I get out of this?!’ And then you say something like, ‘You know, I didn’t connect with the character as much as, someone else is going to really, uh …’ But I didn’t have to do any of that. I read the script and I thought, ‘Wow, this is beautiful.'”

Jacobson voices the lead role of Sarah, who at the time the story starts in 1999 is a young artist living in San Francisco, 3,000 miles from her family, exploring a new relationship with a character voiced by Samira Wiley. “I’m playing a real person, who I got to meet. And I read her book, and so that carried a lot of weight for me,” Jacobson Said. A “rarity,” she added, were voice recording sessions that involved multiple cast members performing together. “To get to be in a room doing the voiceover with them is such a different experience. You get to look the other actor in the eye and you get to act and be present. And with a story that’s really important. There’s a lot going on, and it felt so connected, same as the comedic moments. It’s always better when you’re in the room.”

Monday’s event at Regal Union Square was presented by Hilarity for Charity, a not-for-profit founded by the Rogens to support families affected by Alzheimer’s. Joining cast members were Leah Nelson, the film’s director, producer and co-writer; and Levitt, who co-wrote the screenplay. Guests also included former AMC Global Media CEO Josh Sapan, whose company, Sapan Studios, teamed with IFC on the acquisition. (Sapan knows plenty about lengthy film productions, having fostered the Oscar-winning Boyhood during his AMC tenure.) Current AMC Global execs were also out in force, among them CEO Kristin Dolan, Chief Content Officer Dan McDermott; and IFC Entertainment Group chief Scott Shooman.

Michael Jackson’s ‘Thriller’ Scares Up 40th Week at No. 1 on Top R&B/Hip-Hop Albums Chart

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The set's title track sparks gains for the landmark album after its annual Halloween surge.

For the second time this year, Michael Jackson’s landmark album Thriller glides to the top of the Top R&B/Hip-Hop Albums chart after a mass pop-culture moment.

Unlike the wider spring and summer cycle sparked from the Michael biopic, the latest surge largely centers on one song, the album’s title track, after gains from the Halloween holiday.

Thriller, on the Epic/Legacy record labels, tops the chart dated Nov. 14, rising three spots to crown the list. For its latest coronation, the set earned 55,000 equivalent album units for the Oct. 29 – Nov. 5 tracking week, according to Luminate, an 89% improvement from 29,000 units in the week prior.

Streaming activity contributed 32,000 units of the sum (a figure representing 34.2 million official on-demand streams of the album’s songs), traditional album sums provided 22,000 units and 1,000 units were from track-equivalent album units. (One unit equals the following levels of consumption: one album sale, 10 individual tracks sold from an album, or 1,000 paid/subscription tier or 2,500 ad-supported tier official on-demand audio and video streams for a song on the album.)

With its return, Thriller achieves a 40th week at No. 1 on Top R&B/Hip-Hop Albums, following two weeks at No. 1 in May in the wake of post-Michael mania. The landmark 1982 release, often cited as the best-selling album of all time, held the record for most weeks at No. 1 on the chart from 1984 until 2025, when SZA’s SOS took the title. Thriller narrows the gap with SOS, which remains in first place with 46 weeks on top, having most recently topped the ranking in January.

Here’s a review of the longest leaders on Top R&B/Hip-Hop Albums:

46, SOS, SZA, 2022-2640, Thriller, Michael Jackson, 1983-84; 202629, Please Hammer Don’t Hurt ‘Em, M.C. Hammer, 199026, Just Like the First Time, Freddie Jackson, 1986-8723, Can’t Slow Down, Lionel Richie, 1983-8420, Songs in the Key of Life, Stevie Wonder, 1976-7720, Street Songs, Rick James, 198120, Shoot for the Stars Aim for the Moon, Pop Smoke, 2020-21

Additionally, Thriller’s new frame gives the King of Pop a 101st career week at No. 1 on Top R&B/Hip-Hop Albums, the second-most of all acts since the chart’s launch in 1965. He trails only former Motown labelmates The Temptations, who have posted a record 109 weeks in the penthouse.

Elsewhere, Thriller crowns the Top R&B Albums chart for an 18th week with a 3-1 ascent, while it races 13-2 on the all-genre Billboard 200. On the latter, it achieves its highest rank since 1984, the year it wrapped its 37 weeks atop the list, the most for an album by a single artist.

Gochman Collection Builds a Home for Native Art in Westchester

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The private collection opened its Branchline location in Katonah as a way to bring Native art into public view, though questions linger about its engagement with the local Lenape community.

KATONAH, NY — In 1680, English colonists bought the land that would become Bedford, New York, from a Munsee Lenape sachem named Katonah, meaning “great hill.” Today, his name belongs to a Westchester village that is now home to Branchline, the Gochman Family Collection’s (GFC) new gallery and studio space, which opened to the public on November 6 with Line Break, an exhibition of works drawn primarily from its holdings of contemporary Native American art.

Since its founding in 2021, GFC has collected over 800 works with support from Native and non-Native gallerists, artists, and curators alike, including Creative Director and Curator Rachel Martin (Tlingít), curator and Hyperallergic contributor Shándíín Brown (Diné), and Director Laura Phipps, who also serves as Branchline’s director. Designed by Elizabeth Roberts Architects, the new space occupies two historic storefronts steps from Katonah’s Metro-North station, with free-admission galleries, skylit artist studios, a community ceramics studio, and apartments for invited artists.

Branchline makes a public home for what has essentially been a lending operation: According to GFC, more than 260 of its works have appeared in over 100 exhibitions at institutions including the Metropolitan Museum of Art, the Whitney Museum of American Art, and The Broad, and it charges no loan fees. That model speaks to a known problem with private collecting, explained Joseph Pierce (Cherokee), professor and founding director of the Native American and Indigenous Studies Initiative at Stony Brook University. “Just because you collect something doesn’t mean you’re going to show it,” Pierce told Hyperallergic. “The risk is that it doesn’t circulate.”

Create a free account to read more articles each month, and get the best of Hyperallergic in your inbox.

This residency at the U-M Stamps School, in partnership with the U-M Museum of Art (UMMA), offers artists a $20,000 honorarium, housing, studio space, and a culminating exhibition in UMMA’s prominent Stenn Gallery.

The Bayeux tapestry, a new biography of Edward Hopper, The Met's exhibition catalogue on Krasner and Pollock, what architecture is, and more

Funding for tuition, healthcare, and stipends is available. Apply to the University of Maryland, Baltimore County program by Feb 1 to be considered for financial support.

The 10 Most Intense Movie Scenes of All Time, Ranked

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Looking over some of the most intense movie scenes of all time is also going to reveal a bunch of movies that themselves can be counted as the most intense of all time. Overlap’s inevitable, but the focus below will be on those individual scenes. Sure, a scene can be long, taking up 10 to 20 percent of a movie’s runtime in some cases, or if it’s suspenseful for a shorter amount of time, maybe it’s an especially harrowing two to three minutes.

Also, talking about specific scenes will mean spoilers can be found below, but not every scene here necessarily takes place near a film’s conclusion. And for one more “also,” these movies have all been out for at least a few years, and most are quite well-known, so you're unlikely to have too much genuinely spoiled here, if you're interested enough in movies overall to read (or skim) a ranking like this.

Since 2027 is less than two months away, at the time of writing, Children of Men might well be an even more stomach-churning watch nowadays than it might've been back in 2006. Most dystopian movies are intense in one way or another, for hopefully obvious reasons, but the bleak future (which isn't a future for much longer) of Children of Men feels especially so.

The first scene is shocking, as are two set pieces that involve a car (one of them in the car, and one involving a car that doesn’t start), yet the final sequence, which sees the surviving characters (not too many) caught up in an all-out war zone, is the most full-on in Children of Men. It contains the most elaborate of the movie’s long takes, and it does an uncomfortably good job at making you feel like you're caught up in all the horror and bloodshed yourself.

There was a time when the shower scene in Psycho might've been genuinely surprising, but nowadays, you know, it’s the shower scene from Psycho. It comes before the halfway point of the movie, and it features the only character who’s been in the entire movie, scene by scene, up until that point. And out of nowhere, she’s murdered in a way that was very shocking by the standards of the early 1960s.

It’s a real shock to the system, or would've been before it became legendary, and so for all that, this scene’s well and truly earned its reputation.

It’s about as famous as thriller movie scenes get, and it played a part in rewriting the rules of what you could and couldn’t do early on in a movie. It’d be shocking enough to have the shower scene happen at the end, but when it happens where it does here, it’s like, “Wow, there’s an hour left, and now literally anything can happen… and actually, what even can happen?” It’s a real shock to the system, or would've been before it became legendary, and so for all that, this scene’s well and truly earned its reputation as an all-time great moment in cinema history.

Alfred Hitchcock got a shout-out here by highlighting Psycho, and then David Fincher, who’s also got a knack for directing suspenseful and effortlessly intense movies, is going to be mentioned shortly. In similar territory is Brian De Palma, and it feels necessary to highlight at least one scene he’s done as being especially intense, so of all the candidates (often finales; see Carrie and Scarface), here’s the big shootout in The Untouchables.

Well, it’s kind of big. The gunfight itself doesn't technically last too long, but The Untouchables spends an agonizingly long time building it all up, and then the use of slow motion during the build-up and when the shooting itself starts also draws the scene out in the best of ways. It’s such an expertly done set piece, and even if De Palma might have some movies that are darker and more consistently tense than The Untouchables, it’s this single scene that best demonstrates his knack for crafting cinematic suspense.

Make no mistake: pretty much the entirety of Zodiac is tense, and then when it’s not tense, it’s more disquieting and despairing. The search for the Zodiac Killer is at the core of the story, unsurprisingly, and most of the tense parts play out when the killer himself is at large, with the more subtly uneasy parts being found in the back half, when the chances of actually finding the killer seem to dissipate further scene by scene.

The terror does come back in full force, though, with the infamous basement scene in Zodiac, which happens closer to the end and involves Robert Graysmith possibly encountering the killer, but the not knowing – and the darkness of the sequence visually – bring the film the closest it gets to outright horror territory. It’s far from the only memorable scene in Zodiac, of course, but it does leave the biggest impact.

The scene you probably remember as being the opening one, even if Saving Private Ryan does start decades after the end of World War II before flashing back, it’s that famous extended sequence depicting the D-Day Landing at Omaha Beach. Guess it is chronologically the first scene that takes place in Saving Private Ryan, and it does have such an impact that it sticking in one’s mind over the actual opening scene is understandable.

There is also a large-scale battle sequence near the end of Saving Private Ryan, and that’s no slouch in terms of showing some truly visceral combat either. However, it's this opening sequence that proves most startling, coming earlier in the film, and it’s also a depiction of an actual battle, so it has that extra sense of dread and terror to it (the final battle is fictionalized, though it does aim to convey World War II-era combat nonetheless).

There are pictures here. You need pictures in an article, apparently. There was an effort made with all the other movies to source relevant pictures (you know, not just showing any moment from Saving Private Ryan or Psycho, but screenshots that convey the scene in question and the relevant intensity). With The Silence of the Lambs, though, the notably tense sequence – or at least the most intense part of that sequence – is memorably very dark, and it’s harder to make things out when it’s not in motion.

It involves Clarice Starling finally confronting Buffalo Bill, and eventually thwarting him, but not until after he switches off all the lights and stalks her around his already creepy house/basement while wearing night-vision goggles. It’s perhaps the part when The Silence of the Lambs most feels like a horror movie, though any big scene with Hannibal Lecter is, quite naturally, also frightening (an honorable mention should go out to his big escape sequence).

You do know this one is coming at all points, and sure, there is even some relief when it happens, just because 127 Hours so perfectly captures – and makes you feel – the utterly nightmarish situation its central character is in throughout. He is hiking without sufficient company and safety equipment, and ends up trapped when a boulder falls on his arm, crushing it and then ultimately not being movable.

So, after several days, he makes the decision to amputate his arm with the very few pieces of equipment he has nearby, and it is what saves his life, sure, but it does also prove nauseating to watch because nothing is shied away from. It’s one of the most painful-to-watch scenes in relatively recent memory, yet it has to be, and there is an understandably great sense of relief alongside the absolute nausea you'll feel witnessing it all.

Jaws is another Steven Spielberg movie that, like Saving Private Ryan, technically begins near or on a beach, and also in a manner that proves surprising and famously intense. That first shark attack scene is monumental, as are a bunch of the sequences that follow throughout the first hour or so of Jaws, though the final act is where the movie obviously crescendos, and if you want to call it the “final showdown,” then that’s it. That’s what’s going here.

You’ve got a whole movie of things not really letting up, and it’s still all quite patiently done, not to mention in a manner that makes being truly invested in that climax inevitable. Jaws is – and forever will be – the best movie about three guys who have to go out to sea and kill a shark, and this extended climax is a big reason why.

Everything in Inglourious Basterds is pretty great, and it’s also a film that has a handful of standout scenes that all manage to feel incredibly tense, but usually in different ways, so there’s no real repetition here. It’s longer than a fair few Quentin Tarantino movies, albeit paced in a way that really makes it all fly by, and all the big scenes help immensely in that regard.

That does also make highlighting one over all the others tough, but here, the one that’s going to be singled out is the tavern sequence. All the other show-stopping scenes are perfect and 10/10-worthy, with this one, like, being an 11/10 scene. Movies don’t get much more nerve-wracking than this, and it’s a shame you can only ever see something for the first time just the once, since being able to experience a scene this perfectly surprising again would be wonderful.

There’s no shark here, but Whiplash does a similar thing to Jaws in terms of having suspense and unease pretty much right from the start, and then having those feelings become more intense as things progress. You're not given a lot of time to breathe, and the dynamic between a dedicated student and a tyrannical instructor gets impossibly uncomfortable, not to mention psychologically harrowing, continually reaching new heights… or lows?

Anyway, that does result in the climax being the biggest and most jaw-dropping sequence in the movie. Whiplash also twists the knife after it’s over, since the movie leaves you hanging in terms of the implications of the eventual moment of triumph, or tragedy. Or it could be a bit of both. It’s hard to know. You'll never know. So you're on the edge of your seat for the final 10 to 15 minutes of Whiplash, and then you continually feel queasy for the next 10 to 15 years after Whiplash came out, whenever you think about it and its ending. That is undoubtedly impressive stuff for a movie’s ending to do.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

We might get an early taste of Ascendance before long.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Capcom still hasn't announced a release date for Monster Hunter Wilds: Ascendance, the Sunbreak-scale DLC that was revealed in February, but it seems like we might be getting a chance to sample the expansion before too long. A recent Steam listing update has provided a strong reason to suspect that there'll be a public beta for Ascendance available ahead of its launch in 2027.

As SteamDB changelogs show, the Steam app entry for Monster Hunter Wilds' 2024 public beta was quietly updated early this morning to change the app's name from "Monster Hunter Wilds Beta test" to "Monster Hunter Wilds: Ascendance Beta test." Similarly, the listing's visual assets were updated to feature key art of a hunter facing an Arateka, one of the new monsters being introduced in the expansion.

And sure enough, while the Wilds beta was removed from player libraries after it ended, it's possible for users who'd previously played it to confirm its listing changes within the Steam app by viewing their own played games from their Steam profile. I just looked. It says Ascendance right there.

As the previous Wilds beta consisted of a repackaged demo build, it's likely that an Ascendance beta would follow suit: I'd expect a similar set of hunts to what in-person attendees were able to get hands-on time with at Tokyo Game Show in September, offering a chance to try out the new Boost Bracer weapon mechanics in Master Rank combat.

If a beta does materialize, it'll hopefully be a smoother experience than what beta hunters got in 2024. The early Wilds playtest was the first time PC players encountered the dreaded origami monsters, a symptom of the severe optimization issues that tanked Wilds' reputation for over a year after launch.

As for when that beta might be available, we can only speculate—but given that Monster Hunter has made prior appearances at Geoff Keighley productions, a public playtest that accompanies a hypothetical Game Awards trailer makes intuitive sense. But I'm just reading the tea leaves here—Capcom hasn't said a word about when it'll offer its next DLC teaser.

Monster Hunter Wilds: Ascendance is set to launch some time in 2027.

Why Joshua Jackson Was Only Dawson's Creek Star Missing From Memoir

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

In Kerr Smith’s new memoir I Don’t Want to Wait: My Life on Dawson’s Creek, Joshua Jackson was not interviewed like this fellow cast—and Smith shared why in an exclusive E! News interview.

Kerr Smith didn’t want to wait to tell his Dawson’s Creek story.

And so, fans of The WB series might notice that while Katie Holmes, Michelle Williams and James Van Der Beek sat for interviews for his new memoir I Don’t Want to Wait: My Life on Dawson’s Creek, Joshua Jackson did not return to Capeside—and that wasn’t Smith’s decision.

“All I want to say is everybody makes a choice about whether or not they want to be part of the project or not,” Smith told E! News in an exclusive interview. “Some did, some didn't.”

And Smith appeared to nod to Jackson’s hesitation to participate in a parenthetical within the book itself, which hit shelves today, Nov. 10, while recounting that scene between Holmes’ Joey and Jackson’s Pacey in the series finale.

While pushing back on Holmes’ admission that she didn’t love her performance, Smith revealed Jackson wasn’t available to share his perspective.

“Allow me to weigh in with my expert analysis. (Yep, I’m now an expert. I mean, have you not heard? I wrote the book on this stuff.),” Smith wrote. “With all due respect, you’re wrong. You positively crushed this scene. Both of you. Right, Josh? Josh? (I’m sure he’ll get back to me any second now.)”

E! News reached out to Jackson’s rep for comment but hasn’t heard back.

But while Smith and Jackson didn’t go down memory lane, or rather creek, together, the Final Destination alum emphasized what it meant to him to revisit series with the rest of the cast and crew.

“I wanted to get everybody else’s memory on it too,” Smith told E!. “I did over two dozen interviews for the book so I wanted to get everybody’s experiences and put it in one place to understand what a great show this is.”

When it came to his conversations with Holmes, the 54-year-old mused that it was easy for them to simply snap right back into the late 1990s, adding, “It’s like we never even lost a step.”

“You make those bonds back then and you just kind of hang on to them,” he added. “It doesn’t matter how many years have gone by.”

And, of course, it was especially sentimental for Smith to get to reminisce with Van Der Beek before his death earlier this year after a three-year battle with colorectal cancer.

“When I did interview James, he was already pretty sick,” Smith noted. “But he was kind enough to give me a few hours and we had an amazing discussion.”

In addition to being a reflection on Dawson’s Creek as a whole, Smith also included a chapter for former cast and crew to share their memories with Van Der Beek following his death.

“I wanted to give everybody that knew him back then a chance to say anything at all if they wanted to,” Smith shared. “I just thought it was really nice. I think it’s something that he would’ve been OK with.”

As he put it, “I think it’s a good way to end the story.”

You can read the entire fantastic, Steven Spielberg-film worthy story in Smith’s new memoir, and keep reading for even more secrets into the beloved teen drama…

While Fox was first to pick up Dawson's Creek, it subsequently dumped the teen drama.

"I was told they were struggling with Party of Five and they didn't need another one," series creator Kevin Williamson said at the 2015 ATX TV Festival.

But that paved the way for The WB to pick it up two years later.

While Paula Cole's "I Don't Want to Wait" became the iconic Dawson's Creek theme song, producers originally wanted Alanis Morissette's "Hand in My Pocket."

Alas, they couldn't land the rights, what a jagged little pill.

Dawson. Pacey. Joey. Not names you hear every day. So how did Williamson come up with them?

"Dawson came from a real place called Dawson's Creek where we all hung out as kids and partied," he told The Hollywood Reporter in 2018, while Pacey "came from a friend of a friend named Pacey and I'd never met anyone with that name and thought it was a cool name."

And for the show's heroine, Williamson "wanted Josephine as a very girly name that could easily turn into a tomboy name like Joey."

Remember Dawson's infamous "walk the dog" line in the pilot?

Williamson inserted the euphemism because the network would not let them say the word "masturbate" in 1998.

Before Katie Holmes was cast in the role that would make her America's sweetheart-next-door, Blair was this close to landing the role.

"Joe was written to be a tomboy and everyone was coming in being very much a tomboy. We were very close to going with Selma Blair, who was amazing," Williamson told THR. "She read it very tough, with a lot of heart."

However, once he saw Holmes on tape, with "those two big eyes," Williamson knew they had their Joey.

Williamson liked Joshua Jackson so much, he didn't care what role the Mighty Ducks alum played.

"I fell in love with Josh Jackson because he could read any role, Dawson or Pacey," he told THR. "But something wasn't complete and that's when the network said they didn't see Josh as Dawson, and rightfully so. So, I went, 'OK, he's Pacey,' because I knew I wanted him in the show no matter what."

Charlie Hunnam Auditioned, Won Role of Husband

An 18-year-old Charlie Hunnam read for the WB drama in 1999 and, while he didn't land the part, he did meet Katherine Towne at his audition.

They tied the knot in Las Vegas three weeks later and divorced in 2002, "three terrible, painful, expensive years later," as Hunnam put it to the Associated Press in 2017.

Dawson's Creek became so popular that American Eagle inked a deal in 2000 to outfit the actors.

The characters wore mostly AE clothes throughout season three, and the cast appeared in ad campaigns and promos for the clothing line, helping drive the company's net income above $105 million in 2001.

When James Van Der Beek was first cast, the studio wanted him to change his hair.

"We found an ad for The Devil's Own, the movie with Brad Pitt," he recalled to The Daily Beast in 2012. "They said: 'What about Brad Pitt's haircut?' That's how I got my season one haircut."

Holmes dated Jackson early in the show's run, before Pacey and Joey realized they opposite-of-hated each other.

"I'm just going to say that I met somebody last year," Holmes told Rolling Stone in 1998, not denying that the somebody was Jackson. "I fell in love, I had my first love, and it was something so incredible and indescribable that I will treasure it always. And that I feel so fortunate because he's now one of my best friends. It's weird, it's almost like a Dawson-and-Joey type thing now."

Plus one more nugget: "He's been in the business so long," she added, "and he's really helped me. I respect him as a friend and a professional."

When Kerr Smith first signed on to join the show as Joey's new boyfriend, he had no idea the character would eventually come out and be part of the first-ever gay kiss on TV.

"I always knew I wanted Jack to come out of the closet, but I didn't even tell [Smith]," Williamson said at the ATX TV Festival. "Let the audience love him, then let's have him come out of the closet and have Joey have to deal with that—and then that would eventually drive her back to [Dawson]."

More poignantly, Williamson has said that every character on the show inherited traits from him, including Jack.

“Every single character has a trait of me in them,” he told Entertainment Weekly in 2018. “I had just, in my 20s, gone through the coming-out process and had told my parents I was gay. I had taken that whole journey, and I wanted a character on the show to represent that journey and to represent that side of me."

Jack's coming out episode, cowritten by Williamson and producer Greg Berlanti, was inspired by true events.

"A friend of mine had a story that I brought in that he got outed accidentally," Berlanti told EW, "because he wrote a love poem that it was so clear to everyone else that it was about a guy.”

Michelle Williams and Busy Philipps' best-friendship, which first blossomed on Dawson's Creek, is still going strong to this day.

“I’m so in love with her,” Williams told People of her bestie at a 2016 screening of Manchester by the Sea, one of many red carpet date nights for the besties. “She’s proof that the love of your life does not have to be a man! That’s the love of my life right there.”

Philipps is also godmother to Williams' daughter Matilda Ledger, who was born in 2005.

Dawson finally losing his virginity was such a big show moment that Van Der Beek brought a very special gift to set the day the scene was filmed.

"I remember bringing champagne for the crew—Dawson finally lost his virginity!" he told The Daily Beast.

Before starring on One Tree Hill, Chad Michael Murray had an arc on Dawson's Creek playing Charlie, a love interest for Jen and Joey.

But while Charlie got around, Murray didn't seem to make a lot of friends during his first stint in North Carolinav (OTH later filmed there).

During a Paley panel in 2009, Philipps called him "a douche," later saying, "Don't worry. I'm not real worried about burning bridges with CMM."

Van Der Beek added, "He's actually come a long way." Post-event, Philipps tweeted, "Just finished the dawson's creek panel. Maybe I was too harsh on chadM2… Nah."

Murray, meanwhile, hasn't stirred the pot, saying in 2015 that he learned a lot from the experience.

“I had the opportunity to learn from people who had been doing it for a couple of years longer than I was when I walked into the Dawson’s cast,” he told Cosmpolitan.com in 2015. “You know, Michelle and James and Katie and Josh—they really kind of guided me, because they’d already been around for five, six years, maybe longer. And if they had the time, they were showing me the ropes. I had no idea what lighting was, I had no idea about a lot of the technical aspects of what we do, and so I’m grateful for that experience.”

Just a sampling of the famous faces, ranging from the already familiar to future huge stars, who appeared on Dawson's Creek over the years: Seth Rogen, Jane Lynch, Julie Bowen, Scott Foley, Jensen Ackles, Hilarie Burton, Rachel Leigh Cook, Ali Larter and Oliver Hudson.

Young Americans was a short-lived spinoff centering on Pacey's friend Will Krudski (Rodney Scott) and his boarding school classmates at Rawley Academy.

Also starring Ian Somerhalder and Kate Bosworth, it was canceled after just one season.

The Show Almost Ended With Joey Picking Dawson

The show's main love triangle officially disassembled when Joey chose Pacey in the 2003 series finale.

But it almost didn't turn out that way, as Williamson first envisioned Dawson getting the girl. But halfway through writing the finale, Williamson realized that a happy ending for that couple wasn't "what the show was set to be."

"I wanted it to be a twist on the teen genre but also wanted it to be surprising, honest and real and say something about soul mates and what soul mates can be," he explained at the ATX TV Festival. "That's why we did it that way. When you left the show in that last moment, they're a family and everyone got what they wanted. There was fulfillment and they were all happy."

At the last minute, he changed his mind and the rest is TV history. But Williamson admitted his mom, who played for Team Dawson, was not happy.

In the finale, which jumped ahead five years, the group had to deal with the devastating loss of Jen.

"Dealing with the death of one of their own was the final thing that thrust them into adulthood forever. Dawson's Creek was a coming-of-age story and that was the idea behind that ending," Williamson explained of the decision. "That's why we killed Jen, because I wanted them to deal with a death of one their own as that final lesson."

Some of the executive producers who get their start on Dawson's?

Julie Plec (who went on to gift us with The Vampire Diaries and The Originals), Berlanti (Everwood, Arrow, The Flash, Riverdale...the list goes on and on, and that doesn't even include his movies), Rob Thomas (Veronica Mars and iZombie), Jenny Bicks (Sex and the City), Anna Fricke (Being Human), Dana Baratta (Jessica Jones) and many more.
